The official gazette of Belgrave Harriers

Number 229

All colour sports photography in this issue by Mark Shearman

Front Page: Matt Douglas always gives everything in his races - whether League, Championship or Olympic Games. The year 2000 saw him take his best 400 metre hurdles time below 50 for the first time and he ended the season second only, in Britain, to our own Chris Rawlinson, and well into the World's Top 50.

Inside Back Page: Top left - As the finish nears in the final of the Olympic 100m, Dwain Chambers is inches ahead of USA's Jon Drummond but both just missed the medals. Top right - Chris Rawlinson reached the semi-final stage of the 400m hurdles but was disappointed not to get into the final. Bottom left: Moving up to another level in 2000, one-lap racer Sean Baldock ran 45.20 in the AAA but ran into Mr. Johnston in his Olympic heat. Bottom right - Darren Campbell celebrated Olympic silver after a brilliant run in the Olympic 200 metres where he became third fastest Briton of all time.

Advertisement · page 2

40% Off The Joining Fee At One Of The UK's Premier Health & Fitness Clubs.

David Lloyd

Belgrave Harriers have joined up with David Lloyd Raynes Park, to offer its members an opportunity to join one of the most exclusive clubs in the UK.

40% Off The Joining Fee At One Of The UK's Premier Health & Fitness Clubs.

It's that time of year again when intentions of a self improved lifestyle begin with a committed passion. However we all know how hard it is to keep that intention at the forefront of our life in later months. At David Lloyd we recognise this trend and to help you stay passionate about the improved changes within your life, we offer you the complete solution to a healthier lifestyle. At Raynes Park we boast one of the largest gymnasiums in Europe, it provides the ultimate exercise environment, fully air-conditioned with up-to date training apparatus. If you do find your motivation lacking, personal training sessions can be arranged. Although to get fit does require some physical exertion, relaxation and recuperation are also beneficial to the new you. Therefore with this in mind, spacious changing rooms will allow you to relax in comfort and enjoy a refreshing sauna, Nordic steam room, Jacuzzi or hot shower. Our fitness suite changing rooms include a mixed sauna and steam room, sunbeds, showers and TV relaxation areas. With a heated 25m indoor pool it is the ideal way to exercise the body and mind, and to complement this a heated 20m outdoor pool with sun terrace can be enjoyed throughout the year. A range of specialist treatments are available including hair salon (unisex), beauty therapy centre and injury treatment clinic.

For further details or to book an appointment to visit the club please contact the sales team at David Lloyd Raynes Park. Tel: 020 8543 8020.

Letter · page 3

Dear Belgravians,

Mark Sinclair, Marketing Manager, Fila UK Ltd.

It gives me great pleasure to at last write something again for The Belgravian, although now as the voice of the Sponsor, FILA, and not as an athlete and member. The last time I contributed I submitted a short series of cartoons set around the much loved and missed Tom Carter, and these were included by the then editor, Mr Bill Laws.

Like the Belgravian, which is by far the best club magazine I have ever seen, Belgrave continues to move on and on. As an ex-athlete, and Marketing Manager for Fila UK I am constantly reminded of what a superb example of team work the club really is. Of course, many people in the sport are envious of you. They believe that the club succeeds because of its financial security. I know, and you know, that they need to look deeper. I am old enough to remember the days where Belgrave leaped from league to league with only a handful of competitors as a result of great atmosphere, camaraderie, and hard training. And from what I have seen recently, nothing has changed except the names. The club's financial security has come from an extremely professional Team Management married to the Belgrave Team Spirit and little else. Other clubs must look harder to find out what it is that makes our club the best in the Country and one of the best in Europe.

I am extremely proud of the club and FILA are proud to be involved as Sponsor. The Club embodies what club athletics must be about in this new Century. Please accept our best wishes to you all for this year, especially the Ladies Team, who are undoubtedly going through a significant stage in the club's growth and about to make history!

Sincerely yours,

Mark Sinclair, Marketing Manager, Fila UK Ltd.

Mark SinclairTom CarterBill Laws

Editorial · page 3

We wholeheartedly thank FILA UK Ltd. for their continued sponsorship of our club. They help in many ways and as far as The Belgravian is concerned they have kindly purchased computer software for us which will enable your Editor to carry out a lot of the tasks that are normally done at the print shop and thus help us to keep costs down.

This edition also marks another milestone in the magazine's development with a full page advertisement by David Lloyd Leisure Ltd. who are offering reduced membership to Belgrave Harriers. If you are thinking of joining a health club then get on down to the David Lloyd Club at Raynes Park - and if you haven't thought about it, maybe you should! Don't forget to tell them that you are a member of Belgrave Harriers and take your copy of the magazine with you to show that you are eligible for reduced membership.

Many of you contribute towards the funding of our magazine by adding that little extra donation when your club subscriptions are due. Please be assured that your help is much appreciated. And to that most generous, anonymous benefactor who has offered to bear the bulk of the cost of this, our second issue of the 2000-2001 club year, giving us the chance to break into a "two issues per year" cycle, we offer our heartfelt thanks.

Editorial · page 3

As the Britain considers the implications of closer political and economic ties with Europe, Belgrave Harriers is strengthening its links with athletic competition on the mainland in a big way. Last summer our senior men's team won Group B of the European Champion Clubs competition in Liege, Belgium, and are now relishing the chance of taking on the giants of Group A in 2001. It will be a huge task to compete successfully against teams that are virtually national squads or sponsored by multinational companies but the dream is that one day Belgrave Harriers will become champions of Europe. At the time of writing it is believed that this year's competition will be held in Madrid on the last weekend in May.

And for the first time the seniors will be joined in European competition by Belgrave's junior men's team who will also be representing the United Kingdom against the best under 20 teams from across the channel. All credit to John Powell's Team Managership that has taken our young men to this stage. As John retires to take on other roles in the sport, Paul Ireland takes over to lead our team to, we hope, even greater success.

An explosion is taking place in our women's teams. UK Athletics has announced plans to reorganise certain team competitions from 2002 onwards and in particular to combine some men's and women's teams. In recognition of this, Belgrave Harriers has produced plans to meet both this developing situation and the future needs of the sport. As part of its Development Programme, Belgrave is supporting its top women members in the same way that it has supported its top male athletes. Part of this support includes help for overseas training and competition. Phil Green, well known as a team manager for the UK and for various club teams has become a Belgravian and is masterminding plans for our top male and female athletes to compete in IAAF Grand Prix II meetings. Many top class female athletes have been attracted by this programme and there is no doubt that we should be able to make a dramatic impact on women's League and Cup competition in 2001. Our aim is for our women's track & field team to reach the final of the UK Women's Jubilee Cup. And the winners of that competition will go on to compete in Europe in 2002 ...

John PowellPaul IrelandPhil Green

Report · page 4

Hon. Secretary's Report to the 2000 Annual General Meeting

C.H. Henn

We heard with sadness during the year of the deaths of Charlie Jones, Frank Jarvis and Win Shepherd, who were Presidents of the Club in 1959/60,1966/67 and 1990/91 respectively, and also of Don Brown, Daye Donovan, Len Herbert and Eddie Short.

The Club has been honoured by a bequest of more than £4000 under the will of Pamela Lilian Pyer, in memory of her father, Micky Pyer, who was President in 1971/72.

The Committee were approached about a year ago by the Chairman of the Borough of Hounslow Athletic Club and some of his colleagues, with a view to the possible amalgamation of the two clubs. The Committee saw a number of potential advantages in amalgamation, on terms that were agreed after much discussion, though of course everything was subject to the approval of the memberships of the two clubs. In the event there was more opposition within the Borough of Hounslow club than their Chairman had expected, their proposal was withdrawn, and the Extraordinary General Meeting that your Committee had arranged provisionally was called off.

The Committee are developing a child protection policy aimed to ensure that the Club discharges its responsibilities towards its younger members. Though this policy is yet to be finalised in detail - it will be suitably notified to all concerned once it has been - we envisage that there will be (1) a code of practice for coaches, team managers and others who work with Club members under 16 years of age; (2) a register of those coaches and others who have been cleared by the Club for such work after producing a letter of police clearance; and (3) a formal complaints procedure to be followed if, unhappily, it should ever become necessary. The policy will be consistent with guidance which is available nationally. The Committee believe that the Club should set up effective arrangements as soon as possible, and wish in readiness to see a Club Child Protection Officer elected by this AGM.

In athletics terms, the Club has had another highly successful year.

The Club team in the men's National 6-stage road relay in Sutton Park last October finished an excellent fourth in our first appearance at this event. Following the advances of recent times, Belgrave men won the Surrey Cross-Country League for the first time in twenty-eight years, by a close margin over Thames Hare & Hounds - and our under-13s were also overall winners in their age-group. Although our team was narrowly beaten into second place in the South of the Thames 'Junior' cross-country championships, Belgrave won the 'Senior' race for the third successive year. Sixth place in the South of England Cross-Country championships was followed by an excellent sixth place - and first Southern club - in the 'National': indeed, with nine to score Belgrave were fourth club. But bronze medals in the South of England 12-stage road relay in early April were followed by seventeenth place in the National race at the end of the month: it was good that we finished a team, for the first time since 1994, but too many people were unavailable for the Club to finish where it should.

Belgrave's women had a good winter, too. Restored to the first division of the Surrey Cross-Country League, they finished sixth to maintain their place in it; and they won bronze medals in the Surrey Cross-Country championships. It was very good to see a group of our women make up their minds to a good performance in the London Marathon, and after preparing together and running as a team secure the bronze medals - indeed they were the first women's team to close in, and all three scorers recorded considerable personal bests.

In track and field athletics our men's team were once again champions of the First Division of the British Athletics League, for the fourth year running; and a first-rate performance in winning the second division match of the European Champion Clubs Cup meant this country's promotion, so that Belgrave as national champions once again will take part next year in the first division match. However, the dependence of any club on getting people to turn out - something especially difficult, perhaps, in an Olympic year - was illustrated by our lapse to fourth place domestically in the Gold Cup. Our team in Division One of the Men's Southern League, always liable to be 'raided' at short notice to supply our British League team, found the going hard this year but have retained their place. The Club's women finished in the middle of Division Three of the Southern Women's League.

Our under-20 men's team, after winning the Southern Premier Division of the National Junior League, went on to win the national final at Birmingham last weekend; and in the National Junior League Grand Prix final, held in parallel, Tim Abeyie and Scott Thompson finished first and second. Sadly, however, Belgrave have lost their place in the Southern Premier Division of the Young Athletes' League: in the young age-groups, of course, a high proportion of any team moves up into the next age-group each year, so that clubs' standards are volatile, and very vulnerable to any lapse in recruitment.

As always, there have been many outstanding individual achievements. To mention just two individual races: we have seen Dwain Chambers beat Maurice Green, and Chris Rawlinson has established a new world best performance in the, admittedly unusual, 300 metres hurdles event. Eight members of the Club have been selected to represent Great Britain in the Olympic Games that begin next week at Sydney, nearly a fifth of the men's athletics team: Sean Baldock, Darren Campbell, Ben Challenger, Dwain Chambers, Justin Chaston, Matt Douglas, Phillips Idowu and Chris Rawlinson; and Brendan Reilly has been picked for Ireland.

Let me end on a different and longer-term note. The Club's success also depends on Club members being willing to give up time and energy to the jobs that have to be done - the team managers, the coaches, the match officials, those who organise social events for everyone's enjoyment and to generate income, those who maintain this Hall, those who manage the Club itself, and those who help in other ways. We owe all of them our thanks. Without them the Club would not survive, let alone flourish, and without them and people like them in other clubs there would indeed be no organised athletics in this country. For some of these functions it would be an understatement to say that more help is needed.

C.H. Henn, 14th September 2000.

Feature · page 5

Back home again! The Millennium Arena, Battersea Park

Eric Hall

Eric Hall reminds us of previous incarnations of our home track

Now that we have access once again to what has been the Club's track home, on and off, for many years some members have been recalling earlier times and other tracks on that site. One was even heard to remark of the brand new pristine surface that it would probably be all right once the cinders had been spread over it and it had been well churned up after a storm or two!

It seems possible that we first used a track in Battersea Park as long ago as 27th December 1921 for the traditional Boxing Day handicaps. In those days it was a cinder track of three laps to the mile in much the same area as the present one but going out towards the grass banking by the main road. Also in those days competitors were treated to the joy of running through the fumes belted out by Battersea Power Station if there was a down draught at the wrong time! Early in 1922 the Secretary was instructed to write to the London County Council to seek better training and changing facilities. Around the same time it was decided to use St John's Hall, Wimbledon, for winter training so this period can be seen as pivotal to our activities.

At a Committee Meeting on 2nd January 1923 it was reported that no action had been taken by the L.C.C. as to erecting dressing accommodation at Battersea Park and it was decided that the Hon. Secretary should endeavour to obtain further information on this matter. Two months later a letter was received from the L.C.C. noting that the track was now available for use & that the question of a dressing room was under consideration!

By April the changing accommodation had been settled as the Club was requesting that a notice board be allowed! Sounds familiar? In June 1923 Mr R.W. Ricketts suggested that the British Olympic Council be written to and requested to approach the London County Council to reserve the enclosure at the track in Battersea Park for field events and not to set up cricket pitches as then suggested. Nothing further is recorded about this initiative. At the same meeting a letter from the L.C.C. granting permission to erect a marquee was read and action in this matter was left in the hands of a sub-committee and the decision was taken that the marquee should remain until the end of September.

Prior to the use of Battersea Park the Club had used the Price's Candle Co. sports ground at Earlsfield for a number of years and as this was a grass track the Committee noted that adjustments would have to be made to handicap marks as cinder tracks were faster.

Among those with early memories of the track was Past President the late Win Shepherd. She recalled being taken along by her father, Dick Savage, also a Past President. She also believed that her grandfather actually worked in the Park and came home with news of the creation of an athletic track. Among others who were familiar with the track from their early days are Arthur Penny and Bill Lucas.

At the outbreak of the Second World War Battersea was requisitioned and so the Club moved, in 1940, to Tooting Bec for the duration - and a little longer. Even this move did not break Win Shepherd's connection as her husband, Past President Dave Martin-Shepherd, was an active member of the Home Guard who manned an AA gun on the site!

By 1954 the Club was able to return but by now it was a standard track albeit of imperial measurement - that is four laps to the mile! Eventually even a hammer cage appeared after the threatened prosecution of a Club thrower for demonstrating his art without adequate protection of other users. Various refurbishments and conversions took place in the 1960s when it was made metric. Further improvements were made in 1983 when a Spurtan BS synthetic track was laid.

The old track was closed in May 1999 and we now have a new two storey pavilion, a covered stand alongside the home straight, electric timing and an upgraded, very soft, Polytan WS surfaced track consisting of 8 lanes. The total cost of over £2.6m was jointly funded by Wandsworth Council and a Lottery Sports Award. Now officially known as the Millennium Arena it was opened by Tessa Sanderson on 1st June. To many it will still be known as the Battersea Park Track.

As a final note there is no truth in the rumour that the Club demanded completed membership applications from the Red Devil team who helped in the launch of the Capital Cities Meeting - perhaps we ought to have done so as they looked a fairly useful group of high flyers! Sorry about that.

The assembled dignitaries of Wandsworth Council join Belgravians, Track Staff, Surrey representatives and Tessa Sanderson on official opening day - 1st June 2000.

Eric HallR.W. RickettsWin ShepherdDick SavageArthur PennyBill LucasDave Martin-ShepherdTessa Sanderson

Feature · page 6

No ordinary man ...

ARM

John Jeffery, President of Belgrave Harriers 2000-2001

No ordinary man ...

When one finds out that John Jeffery "only" became a Belgrave Harrier in 1992, January 20th to be precise, it seems quite impossible that this man, who is so much a part of every aspect of our Club, can have been with us for less than a decade. And of course it is impossible because in actual fact he has been a part of the Belgrave scene since 1963.

As a 19 year-old, John came to live south of the Thames for the first time to become a Metropolitan Police officer. He had been a member of Southgate A.C. in North London. Now, as a police officer in 'L' Division, he fell in with one of the hardest working group of middle-distance runners in the south-east, all of them police officers and forming part of a formidable track & field team - the Metropolitan Police A.A.C. Among them, Andy Norman was a 440 yards man who was turning his talents towards coaching and team management, John Backley (father of Steve, today's champion spearman) ran half-miles and Dave Prior was a redoubtable competitor over 1 mile and beyond.

John often trained at Battersea Park and it was not long before Tom Carter, with his own squad of runners working out at Battersea, approached him with a view to signing him up. John was interested but fully committed to the Metropolitan Police team. He promised Tom that "one day" he would.

The intervening years saw John blossom as a middle-distance runner. He became British Police Champion at 400m, 800m, 1500m and cross-country. He represented the Southern Counties and A.A.A. and set personal bests of 49.6 for 400m, 1:50.2 for 800m, 3:49.8 for 1500m, 49:50 for 10 miles - which he regards as probably his best ever run over any distance - and even ran a marathon, towards the end of his racing days, in 2:36. He remained close to our Club, always prepared to spend a bit of time with anyone looking for advice on running and training - and for a while entered into a role of official Belgrave coach at Battersea until, during lean times in the '70s, we found we had very few middle-distance track runners left who wanted coaching!

He had a fine athletic career by any standard but John was one of that rare breed who continued to pour his talent and experience into the sport when his own running days had passed. He became the Southern Counties Administrator, a post he held for six years. He took on roles as English and British Team Manager, travelling all over the globe. He coached; he judged; he organised. Was there any aspect of our sport not tackled by John.

True to his word, when his Metropolitan Police days were over, John became a Belgravian. As we have already learned he is not one to do things by halves and becoming a Belgravian, for him, meant taking on responsibility. As Leo Coy, highly successful joint British League team manager, stepped aside to return to his native Ireland, John walked right into the breach. Leo and Bill Laws had taken our track & field team on a white-knuckle ride from Southern League to the very top of the British League. It would take no ordinary man to fill Leo's shoes - but John is far from ordinary. All we can say is that the combination of Bill and John is, today, one of the toughest partnerships in our sport and between them they have consolidated our position as Britain's top male track & field team.

We re-organised our club administration: John was in there giving support. He became a member of our Committee and one of our key men in "Public Relations & Sponsorship". From time to time as the governing bodies and administrators of our sport back themselves from one corner into another, John continues to be approached with offers of high ranking positions in Athletics but he has become an integral part of the Belgrave management team and continues to pack as much hard work for the Club into his days as is humanly possible.

Is there any surprise that we welcome John into the highest office in our Club - President of Belgrave Harriers.

ARM

John JefferyARMAndy NormanJohn BackleySteveDave PriorTom CarterLeo CoyBill Laws

Report · page 6

Members and guests celebrate at Annual Club Dinner

At the Annual Dinner Dance at the Preston Cross Hotel held on 25 November 2000 to celebrate the 113th year of the Club, around 130 members and their guests formally welcomed John Jeffery as the new President of Belgrave Harriers. Special Guests were Mark Sinclair of FILA UK - kit sponsors to Belgrave Harriers, Mike Winch - representing the AAA of England, and John Thresher - until very recently the Chief Executive of Athletics Canada and a Belgrave Harrier himself since 1957. Among other notables present was 92 year-old Arthur Penny - 1934 Empire Games 6 miles Champion. Toastmaster was Past President and 1948 Olympic representative Bill Lucas, DFC and the function was organised by yet another Past President, Ted Pallant.

The Club's major individual trophy winners were announced. The "Tommy Green" Cup for the best performance by a male Belgravian during the preceding year went to Darren Campbell for his run in the Olympic 200 metres final where he won the Silver Medal. The "Centenary" Cup, the equivalent award for women went to Helen Alsop née Maskrey who led the Belgrave team to the bronze medals in the AAA Marathon, held in conjunction with the 2000 London Marathon. Nineteen year-old Scot Thompson was presented with the "Bill Merryman" Trophy for the Young Belgravian of the Year. Discus thrower Scot had played a huge role in the Club's successful campaign to win the National Junior League during the past summer.

The Dinner gives the chance for many Belgravians to renew old friendships and a great many of the Club's outstanding distance runners from the 1960s were present: Gerry North (National Cross Country Champion), Geoff North (AAA 3 miles Champion), Tony Fairclough, Lionel Mann, Alan Black - and of course John Thresher himself who ran an outstanding 13:49.0 for 5,000 metres as long ago as 1965 - on "cinders".

John JefferyMark SinclairMike WinchJohn ThresherArthur PennyBill LucasTed PallantDarren CampbellHelen AlsopScot ThompsonScotGerry NorthGeoff NorthTony FaircloughLionel MannAlan Black

Feature · page 7

Newly Elected Life Members

Rex Bale

Rex Bale Has been a Belgravian for 24 years, since 19th July 1976.

We are always so pleased to find someone who will turn up at an athletics meeting to help but Rex does far more than that - when he attends he takes responsibility for whatever needs to be done. He is respected throughout Surrey as a Field Event Judge and is often called upon to be Field Referee.

Come to any of our home matches and the odds are that Rex will have been there since half past ten or eleven o'clock, sweeping the long jump runways, shifting the sand forward in the pits and calibrating the high jump and pole vault stands. Athletes start arriving from noon onwards and then it's a case of weighing in the throwing implements before the competitions start. Actual judging follows, together with the checking and signing of results cards. At the end of the meeting as competitors drift off home, some seven hours since his tour of duty started, Rex will still be there collecting up the equipment that has been left all over the arena and for which he signed earlier. He is one of that most valuable band that are prepared to travel to away fixtures on our behalf - women's fixtures, men's or youngsters'; Birmingham, Southend, wherever necessary to help the Club fulfil its obligations.

It doesn't stop at officiating either, for Rex is Minutes Secretary for our main Committee. We deal with so much business these days that it is imperative that the records of our meetings are correctly documented and Rex does this admirably. He also acts as our Press Officer for Winter events and is our man at Battersea, very much involved in keeping an eye on what is going on there; he played a vital role as the track was recently being developed.

Like many another who does a fine job no matter what he turns his hand to, Rex is in demand outside our own club and he is Officials Secretary for Veterans A.C. All this and he still enjoys his running.

Tony Fairclough

Tony joined Belgrave in 1959 as one of 'Tom's lads' a group coached by the late Tom Carter and Bob Taylor, which included Jackie Hampshire, Dave Cocks, Alan Morrison, Alan Mead and Chris Steer. He first appeared for the Club in the Belgrave Youths road relay in 1959 and went on to a most successful career at Club and County level. His athletic achievements include:-

- 14th in the Surrey Youths' Cross Country, 1961. - Gaining a Surrey Vest and placing 16th in the Junior Inter-Counties of 1963. - Setting a New Club record on Stage 6 of the London-Brighton Relay, 1963 (record still stands today!) - 4th in the Surrey Junior Cross Country, 1964. - 3rd in the Southern Junior Cross Country, 1964. - 5th in the National Junior Cross Country, 1964. - 19th in the National Junior Inter-Counties Cross Country, 1964. - Being ranked 7th overall in the National Junior Cross Country rankings and gaining an ECCU Vest. - 36th in the National Senior Cross Country, 1967, and being awarded a Club Honours Badge. - Becoming a regular member of Surrey CC and Belgrave London-Brighton Relay teams. - Winning Club Championships at Junior 5 miles CC, Senior 6 miles track, 10 miles CC and 5.75 miles Road Race.

Tony was a Team Managers delight; uncomplicated, reliable and always giving 100% effort. His quiet and unassuming disposition belied the tenacity he applied to his running.

When his competitive career drew to a close, Tony took to officiating, becoming a Grade IV Track Judge in 1990. He has now 'risen through the ranks' to become a highly respected Grade II official. He attends most of our 'home' meetings at Battersea alongside his wife Gillian who is a Grade II Field Events Judge, but does not confine his activities only to the track. During the winter season he appears at cross country and road fixtures, not only for Belgrave but also for County and SEAA events.

Although living at Gillingham in Kent, Tony has been a most loyal Club member over the years and a great ambassador, not only for our Club but for our sport in general.

Rex BaleTony FaircloughTom CarterBob TaylorJackie HampshireDave CocksAlan MorrisonAlan MeadChris SteerGillian

Obituary · page 8

Don E. Brown 1911-2000

EWH

Obituaries

Don E. Brown 1911-2000

We have learned recently of the death of Don Brown in February last. Don E. Brown is noted as a new member in the August/September 1931 issue of the Belgravian. He was one of a batch of notables to whom a welcome was extended at that time. Others were A.G. Bruce; A.E. Taylor; H.W. Shields and the Churcher brothers, Charlie and Harry. Don's name was linked with the latter in race results in the late 1930s. Just turned 20 when he joined us, he soon became a force to be reckoned with in the shorter distance walks. The next issue of the magazine noted that "D.E. Brown impressed in his first race as likely to do very well in the future". He finished 22nd in the Opening 5 mile event of the 1931/32 season with the Churcher brothers among those in front of him. He maintained steady progress throughout the next few seasons in the various Open 7s. moving out to 10 miles he was 6th in the RWA "Junior" in 1933 and was second member of the team led home by Harry Churcher and completed by Alf Harley.

In 1934 Don was third in the AAA's two mile track walk in 13 mins 58 secs., the fastest time then by a Belgravian. Elected Road Captain at the next AGM - no mean feat for one so young - he led by example in both inter-club and Open events and gained his Surrey colours. The next year he improved to second place in the AAA's two miles behind the great A. A. Cooper in the "amazingly fast time of 13 mins 53.8 secs." Two years later Don was third - behind Cooper yet gain. At that time it was said that Cooper was the bane of every track walker and both Harry Churcher and Don had to give him best on a number of occasions despite recording quite exceptional times.

A quieter time followed in the years up to the outbreak of War but even so he finished 13th in the Belgrave Open 7 which indicates that he had great natural ability and although he was only in the "C" team that day they beat the "A" team to take the scratch prizes! He was also noted as a possible for the 1940 Olympic Games. As with so many other races those Games did not take place and the events of September 1939 blighted the careers of many members, Don included. He wrote in 1944 to say that despite a posting in the Sussex Weald he was unable to train.

Post war Don became a highly respected walking judge, a familiar figure at all major races and in the years from 1956 to 1960 he was a frequent visitor to Battersea Park where he did much to assist Stan Vickers and myself in our careers. Don took lap times but made it a rule never to disclose them until the end of the evening. Encouragement was given in a quiet, calm, gentlemanly, manner which he always seemed to apply in any situation. In my mind's eye I still see him at the 200 metre mark advising us when necessary but ever mindful that we had our own views on training and racing programmes. He was a man to be listened to and we appreciated his opinions. He also played an active part in pacing us round Wimbledon Common by cycling at a steady 8 mph with Stan on one side of the rear of the bike and me on the other for 12 miles a session. If you think that sounds easy just try it sometime!

With his concentration on style, of which I am told he was a great exponent, he presented the Club with a silver salver to be awarded annually to the best stylist in the Club 2 mile championship. No one was more delighted than Don when Stan Vickers achieved such great heights in so many races culminating in the bronze medal in the Rome Olympics 1960. A great Belgravian who was walking at the "wrong" time, so far as the standard of competition is concerned, he nevertheless gave of his best whether as a competitor, judge, advisor or friend.

EWH

The caption that goes with this photograph in our archives reads: "Walk Match at Battersea circa 1938. Starters include H.G. Churcher, C.E.Churcher, D.E. Brown, A.A.Harley, F. Jarvis, C.W.Redgrave." Our first stab at actually naming the faces came up with Don Brown (1), Harry Churcher (7), Charlie Churcher (23), C.W. Redgrave (24) and Alf Harley (behind Brown). However, as others have become involved and have cast their eyes over the picture, we are now not at all sure of anyone except Alf Harley. For instance, we are told that Charlie Churcher did not wear glasses and the chap next to Harry, second from the left, may be Jack Rutland If any reader can help us with more specific details of the race then we would be very pleased to hear from them.

Obituary · page 8

Win Shepherd 1925-2000

ARM

Win Shepherd, who died on Sunday 27th August in her 75th year, was the first ever Lady President of Belgrave Harriers, being awarded that honour for the year 1990-91. She had been associated with the club since a toddler. Her father, Dick Savage, had been President in 1944-46 and her late husband Dave Shepherd was also President in 1962-63.

Win became interested in Race Walking at the age of fifteen but as there was no ladies section of the club in those days she became a member of London Olympiads. Win was "Olympiads" club champion on three occasions, Surrey champion for three years and while representing her county in the Inter-Counties won and was placed second four times. Twice she was a member of the champion National Road Walk team. In later years she was also a respected member of another top South London club, Selsonia Ladies A.C.

With her mother, Win organised the refreshments at Belgrave Hall for some nine years and when a ladies section was formed in 1976 she immediately became a full member and the club's first Ladies Secretary. She had been an honorary member for many years beforehand.

Win assisted in the running of all sorts of athletics meetings from youngsters' Thameside Leagues to County Championships, generally working on the results or organising the presentation of trophies. She always had a word of encouragement for the athletes and they in turn respected her as someone who had spent her whole life involved in the sport we love.

Our condolences go to Win's brother Richard, a staunch Belgrave Harrier himself.

ARM

Win ShepherdARMDick SavageDave ShepherdRichard

Obituary · page 9

Frank Jarvis (1907-2000)

GIB & ARM

Obituaries

Frank Jarvis (1907-2000)

It is with much sadness that we have to announce the death from pneumonia of Frank Jarvis aged 93, following a short spell in hospital.

Frank, our oldest member, beating Arthur Penny by just a few months, was born in Hounslow on 12 March 1907, the eldest of three brothers. His father, a regular Grenadier Guardsman, was killed in action in the 1914 War and because of this Frank was unable to complete his education, having to leave school at an early age in order to help support his family. This is hard to reconcile to the man we came to know in later life who was literate, well read and knowledgable about most things and in fact was said to have inherited a silver tongue from his mother, although he claimed it came from kissing the blarney stone

In 1921 Frank nearly became a member of Thames Valley Harriers when invited by that club to join them after a promising showing in a schoolboy cross-country race. But Frank was already involved in the Scouting movement and not ready to participate in another pastime. In years to come he was to play a part in the formation of the Rover Scouts.

In 1928 he joined the Metropolitan Police and for 10 years served with 'C' Division at Vine Street station in London's West End. He then transferred to the information Room at New Scotland Yard, where he served throughout the war and until he retired in 1959.

On leaving the Police he joined the Aliens Department of the Home Office. With his usual enthusiasm and efficiency he became a noted authority on immigration law, much in demand by the courts from whom he received an official letter of commendation for his work in this field.

On joining the Police he soon became a member of the M.P.A.A. cross country team and was also a keen boxer. He took up training with the road walkers of Belgrave Harriers to develop his stamina, joining us in 1935. His interest in this new event became so strong that he soon gave up running and boxing.

He was a tremendous team man. On one occasion he met with an accident at the 20 mile point in a 25 mile race, resulting in a badly cut foot. His team mates were well placed and rather than drop out and let them down, he kept going to close in the team and help them to third place.

A regular competitor in the Police Barking to Southend (36 miles) road race there are two incidents associated with it that Frank always recalled. He claimed to be the only competitor in the history of the race to be pulled out for 'lifting' and the other was on a hot day when a well wisher threw a bucket of cold water over him. Unfortunately his shoes dried out like iron, and he had to be brought home in an ambulance and was off work for two weeks

In 1955 he entered the London to Brighton and back (100 miles), successfully completing the course in 24 hours to become a Centurion.

1958 saw him become Hon. Secretary of the RWA Southern Area, a post he held for many years, he also became Belgrave's Assistant Hon Secretary, Walking Secretary, and Surrey County's Walking Team Manager. On retiring from active competition he served as an official at many events and travelled abroad as a team manager.

Frank always claimed that one of his proudest moments came in 1966, when he was elected President of Belgrave. Looking back we can now see that Frank's Presidency came at one of those pivotal moments in our club. When he became President Belgrave had seen no track or field success to speak of for very many years. This was a period just before the formation of the leagues that we are now used to. “I feel convinced,” he said, “that if the Club was to win ONE trophy, this victory would lead to others.”

His club duly rose to the task and during his term of office was victorious in the ‘Jack Lewis Trophy’, the Club's first major win for 11years. Within weeks they won another even more prestigious event, the ‘HG.Brockman Trophy’. The road to the success that we see today had been started out upon.

In 1974 Frank was made president of the Road Walking Association and in 1974 President of Surrey County AA.

From his first marriage in 1928 Frank had a son Paul and a daughter, Ann. Unfortunately this marriage was dissolved after 19 years, a victim of the war.

He then married Hilda a charming lady who sadly died suddenly, again after 19 years of wedlock.

It was then some 20 odd years ago that he married Val, with whom he spent the remaining years of his life; they were a devoted and loving couple.

To Val, Paul and Ann we send our deepest condolences.

GIB & ARM

Obituary · page 10

W.E. “Bill” Kerr 1932 - 2000

Gordon Biscoe

Obituaries

W.E. “Bill” Kerr 1932 - 2000

Sadly we have to announce the death of Bill Kerr on the 15th December 2000, from a sudden heart attack , aged 68 years.

Bill was born in the Springburn area of Glasgow where his father was a Shipwright. In his early years he did very well at school where he was known as a 'bit of a whiz kid' for his general knowledge. Following his school years he became an apprentice on the railway, but this career was cut short following an accident when the wheel of a steam engine dropped onto his hand, leaving him with the loss of mobility in some of his fingers for the rest of his life.

In Scotland Bill ran for Victoria Park A.A.C, and later on the Anglo Scottish Athletic Club.

However, seeking pastures new, it was in the early 1960s that Bill came south and burst upon the Belgrave scene, going on to carve himself a lifelong niche in the hearts of many of his team mates and training partners of that time.

He was a great acquisition to the Club when we badly needed one.

It is most significant that since hearing of his untimely death, the underlying memory that many of his contemporaries recall was that he was such a likeable, indeed loveable fellow. One who will always be remembered for his singular sense of humour and that mischievous look in his eyes when he related a story.

Perhaps Bill's style and character, as well as his athletic ability, can best be illustrated by recalling the following anecdotes relayed to me by Clive Shippen on hearing the sad news. Clive writes:-

“I well remember going to Belgrave Hall in March 1961 for the ‘five and three-quarters’ road trial - a big race in those days as, generally, the first 12 were selected to carry our colours in the prestigious London to Brighton Relay which was sponsored by the News of the World.

An unfamiliar face was amongst the usual crowd of nervous runners. He had been brought along by Charlie Walker and was introduced to me as Bill Kerr, a Scotsman. Straight away he struck me as being an affable fellow with a sense of humour already apparent, and he was given permission to compete as a guest.

He was a relaxed runner with a long loping stride and he floated along just behind the leaders for most of the race. When he was sure of his way to the finish he eased ahead for a very surprising win. As you can imagine, he had a membership form in his hand before he had barely recovered his breath!

Bill was selected for the long stage 9 of the National April 1961 race. He was not familiar with the race logistics so when the Stage 9 coach dropped off it's 20 competitors and then drove straight on to the end of the stage Bill realised that his nice new Belgrave vest, complete with attached numbers, had gone with it!

I well remember a very agitated Jimmy Green (Editor of Athletics Weekly) running up to me saying 'There's an unknown runner up ahead running in an un-numbered white vest. He took over from Jack Brown so he must be one of your's. Who the hell is he? I can't recognise him. You realise he is likely to get you disqualified'. My heart sank. Somehow we managed to get ahead of the race and notify the judges of Bill's name and number. We were not penalised, and it was an auspicious start for Bill's career with Belgrave.

Bill's best London to Brighton run was in the National event of April 1962 when he recorded 6th fastest time on the long stage 9 with only Kilby, Bullivant, Craig, Freary and Tulloh (all internationals) being faster. What is more he achieved that back in 14th place - one of our bad years.

Bill was very easy going. Nothing rattled him. Perhaps he was too easy going. His sense of humour endeared him to all. On training runs he had a habit of plucking leaves from trees and singing little ditties; alright on training runs, but very occasionally he would do so in races - very disconcerting to the other competitors!

His laid-back, easy going manner did sometimes produce anxiety in team managers. The team for the South of Thames Senior at Maidenhead in 1962 met at Paddington Station. With just 15 minutes to go before departure Bill ambled up saying, 'I'm starving, I must get something to eat'. No sign of nerves there! I suggested that he grab a sandwich to eat on the train. With 4minutes to departure he had still not arrived at the carriage so I rushed to the station buffet fearing there must have been a mishap of some sort. Bill was casually cleaning up a plate of pie and chips! He looked up at me with that mischievous smile and said 'What's the fuss?' "

Bill gained a AAA vest in 1962 and went on to become a Middlesex track champion

Professionally Bill worked, in turn, for the Civil Service; the Anglo Iranian Oil Company as a Project Clerk ,and in latter years as a Processing Clerk for BT. Among his interests can be listed cooking, gardening a love of the music of Peggy Lee and Frank Sinatra and, when away from athletics, hockey.

In 1970 he met Eileen. They were married in 1971, setting up home first in Streatham then Sittingbourne, Kent, before settling down in Caterham where son Fraser was born in 1978.

In 1963 Bill ran in the Scottish National cross country championships and gained his International Vest by finishing 6th. However after the race was over he heard one of his friends bemoaning his fate at having “missed out again” by finishing 7th. Whereupon Bill calmly said, “that's OK you can have my place,” and gave up his Vest in favour of his friend.

Such was the character of the man we will affectionately remember as Bill Kerr

To Eileen, and Fraser, (now studying physics at Oxford University) we extend our deepest condolences

Gordon Biscoe

Bill's best track performances

Obituary · page 11

J.E.C. Rutland

Members Near and Far

J.E.C. Rutland

Since the last issue we have heard of the death of a former member Jack Rutland.

Jack joined us in the early 1930s as a walker and soon made his mark over the shorter distances. He was a member of our National "Junior" winning team in 1936 and put up a number of noteworthy times before the war. War service in the army, mainly in Wales found him linking with fellow Belgravian Percy Wright to foster the sport.

He placed 2nd to Harry Churcher in the inaugural National 10 miles championship in 1947 and was named as an Olympic "possible". Work however intervened and due to a loss of form he was not selected for the 1948 Games although a member of our National winning team that year. He remained a stalwart for a few years putting up good times over all distances until 1952 when pressures from work and family commitments intervened.

J.E.C. RutlandJack RutlandJackPercy WrightHarry Churcher

Feature · page 11

It's a small world on the wide web

Coincidence is a marvellous thing, writes Eric Hall. "Having searched for many years for another copy of a book lent to a 'friend', and not returned, the final resort was to try the 'web'. For those who forecast that the internet will replace book publishing in due course this may seem an odd way of doing things but this time it was a success on two grounds.

The main site for such items was found to be in the US (where else?) and after a very brief search a copy of the long lost volume was located at a reasonable price - not in the US but in New Zealand. Having searched most of the secondhand shops in those islands only a year or so ago this seemed strange but what was to follow was even stranger.

The transaction was done and in a few days the book arrived in deepest Essex. Being so grateful to have found a copy after so long I decided to e-mail the sender with my heartfelt thanks. As the book was about walking training I told him of my connection with the sport and of the fact that I knew the late Norman Read the 1956 gold medallist. Back came a reply to the effect that the dealer in NZ did a bit of jogging now but had run in the UK back in the mid '70s. He said that it was a small world and added that he belonged to a club over here by the name of Belgrave Harriers! My reply was to the effect that he had no idea just how small a world it really was and as his shop was in Christchurch it might be that he knew of Paul or Peter Coughlan. Back came a reply to say that not only did he know the two gentlemen but that he had seen Peter that very afternoon and he sent his regards to all those he met this last year. In addition the book seller, Glenn Hazard, sent his regards to those who remember him and his epic run in the London to Brighton road run back in the mid seventies."

A long time British expatriate sat in his Virginia, USA, home watching TV. Some 30 years previously he had joined the British Army, serving for nine years. Home after that had been, at various times, Perth (Australia), the Middle East (Oman), Malaysia, Paris, the Caribbean, but eventually he settled down in Berryville, Virginia, in the Shenandoah Valley near the Blue Mountains. He runs his own telecommunications company and still travels the world on business but here he was sitting at home watching athletics on American TV when the commentator mentioned that one of the athletes in shot competed for Belgrave Harriers in the UK. Turning to his wife, he mentioned that he too had run for that club as a young man. Memories stirred and it was but a short step from there to switching on the computer and surfing the net to see if the club had a web-site. And there it was ... news ... pictures ... and quite a few names among the contact names that he remembered from earlier days. E-mails followed and before long, on his next trip through the UK, Tony Brooks our one time track & field captain turned up to watch a cross country match and visit Belgrave Hall. It was great to see him and there is even talk about a possible retirement to live in the UK in a few years time. Tony supplied the team photograph that appears on page 9 of this magazine.

Aussie John Dawson picked us up from Tokyo where he is now living and working. He ran with us while still a Junior in the mid-1990s. John went on to clock 1:50 for 800 metres and 48.1 when he returned to Australia. He has struggled with injuries for the past three years but has also qualified as a coach. "I fondly remember my times at Belgrave," he says, "and hope that one day I'll be able to return to England to compete again in claret and gold." John sends his best wishes to all and looks forward to returning to London one day.

Frazer Hazlett has also got in touch. Frazer worked hard to get into our British League team but was another who had a few injury problems. He has turned to Triathlon where he is doing very well - and now lives in Southern California.

Harold Hutchinson competed for the Club in 1936-38 in the sprint hurdles and pole vault. Harold's granddaughter got in touch with us when they found our web-site and now Harold is the proud owner of a club tie that brings him back many memories.

Michael Peacock has e-mailed us from South Africa. He recently ran 49.37 for 400 metres in a pre-season meeting and is hoping to do well in his forthcoming season. He sends his regards to coach Bob Bridges and his old training group, especially Nick Molloy, Dotun Akinbogun, Richard David, Richard Mcmillan, Joe Panton and James Hilston; also to John Jeffrey, Mike Small, Patricia and Alan Mead and of course his good friend Past President Ted Pallant. Accounting studies will keep him busy for the next couple of years but he hopes to return to the UK and wear a Belgrave vest again.

Finally we received communication from a gentleman researching his family history. His grandfather and great-grandfather had both been Belgrave Harriers and he had found us on the web: "Did we have any information regarding them?" Did we! Great-grandfather was Edwin Gordon, Past President and one time Mayor of New Malden; his Great Aunt was Elsie, who is remembered in the name of one of our trophies - the Elsie Cup. Not only did we know about them, of course, but it transpired that our enquirer was still in touch with one of his relations - Douglas Gordon. Douglas was a school friend of our Editor and the two of them joined Belgrave Harriers as 14 year-olds very many moons ago ... Yes, it is indeed a small world!

www.belgraveharriers.com

Obituary · page 11

G.F. Lucas

Another former member who passed away recently was G.F. Lucas. Born in 1914, George joined the Club during war time in August 1943 and was also a member of Gravesend AC. In 1945 he resigned from the Kent Club and ran in Belgrave colours in various events. Possibly his best year was 1948 when he placed 3rd in our 7 miles Cross Country Championship for the "Blackstaffe Shield" behind Len Herbert and Ted Stimpson. He was second man home in our five and three-quarter mile road race in the same year.

Feature · page 11

Prolific Marathoner

There must be quite a few members who remember Chris Fairfield, one of our runners back in the '60s. Chris joined us in 1958 but eventually moved to Stalybridge in Cheshire. All this time he has kept up his Belgrave membership although he now, at the age of 62, runs first claim for East Cheshire Harriers.

In his time he has clocked up an amazing 127 marathons including 18 Londons and 12 New Yorks and has raised over £70,000 for various charities over the last 15 years.

Chris doesn't just stick at marathons either. In February 1998 he trekked to the foothills of Everest for Whizz-Kidz, a charity dedicated to helping provide disabled youngsters with wheelchairs.

Last summer Chris competed in the 20 stage Welsh Castles Relay. He was due to run the 18th stage but got stuck in traffic and eventually arrived for his stint 5 minutes late. Nevertheless he set out on the 9.1 mile leg and worked his way through to finish 40th.

Chris FairfieldChris

Column · page 12

Sidetracks . . .

Members near and far

Sidetracks . . .

Clive Shippen writes regarding a couple of items in the last Belgravian: "I could hardly believe that Mark would feature in a '25 Years Ago' item. That really makes me feel old!

Regarding 'Footnotes', Reebok only really came to the fore after taking over Foster's of Bolton. Foster's had superseded Law's as the foremost made-to-measure shoemaker in England when Sandy Law declined to compromise his principles by getting involved with what he regarded as the poor quality footwear of Adidas and Puma. Peter Driver was Foster's unofficial representative at one time. Foster did a mail-order service to foot outlines and I well remember giving him my own specification for a thick sandwich sole. This was to off-set the blood blisters I acquired on the soles of my feet during road relays. They worked! But unfortunately they also reduced traction; as I found to my cost in a London-Brighton Relay. Thereafter I wore Foster's ultra-light shoes and accepted the blisters!"

It's good to see Maureen and Brian Pritchard attending our functions. Recently Master Thomas Pritchard (born 11/05/1999) was signed up, making him a fourth generation Belgravian. That's got to be a record. Thomas is son of Simon, grandson of Brian and great-grandson of the late Thomas Pritchard who joined 10th June 1924.

And talking of new arrivals - congratulations to Jane and Charles Herrington on the safe arrival of baby daughter Hannah May, born on Thursday 12th October and weighing in at 6lbs 10ozs. We missed Charles at the opening Surrey League race at Brockwell Park but sometimes other things just have to take precedence.

Gordon Doubleday wrote recently and recalled an incident that occurred to a group of Bels: During the mid 1930s a small group of Belgravians decided to spend the Easter holiday walking on the South Downs, staying at those new-fangled havens the Youth Hostels. They started on the Friday with a slog up to the crest of the Downs. Suddenly, in their path was a glider and walking from it came a suitably clad pilot. He approached our lads and said, "Can you chaps run?" He wanted some people to grab the ropes attached to his machine and run, dragging the glider until airborne. Our lads duly complied and, at a signal from the pilot, sprinted along the springy downland turf. He was soon airborne and our lads continued their walk westwards towards Lewes. They paused to take a look skywards. The glider was now well aloft and the pilot gave them a wave of thanks. Could those chaps run? Like Hell they could - and did!

And what of the antics of today's lads. With our running Captain Roger Alsop due to marry Helen Maskrey, our Skipper decided to hold his stag celebrations over the weekend of the Surrey Road Relay which became the first event in the most confusing and violently contested multi-event competition that the participants had ever entered into. Apart from the normal over-indulgences, Go Kart racing resulted in a most spectacular crash involving Bruce Barton and his Cross Country Team Manager (poor Bruce was unable to run for weeks) while the exploits on the golf course saw a Belgravian on an adjacent fairway struck on the rump by a Belgrave impelled ball while all around rolled about helpless with laughter. Charlie Dickinson was victor ludorum while Roger received the contents of a bucket of cold water at the last watering hole of the day. Helen and Roger were duly wed on October 6th at the Registry Office in Morden with the reception held at a recently decorated and immaculate Hall in Denmark Rd., SW19. Needless to say many Belgravians were there to wish them well.

Congratulations to Steve Clarke who has married Kerrie. Kerrie is a Shaftesbury Barnet Harrier but we won't hold that against her. The Clarkes honeymooned in Sydney and had planned things well, taking in Steve Redgrave's gold medal winning effort in the rowing and plenty of Belgrave action in the main Stadium. On one of the Manly ferries across the harbour a fellow traveller commented on Steve's Belgrave track top: "I used to compete for Belgrave Harriers." It turned out to be Graham Seatter who sends his regards to all who remember him.

Our Hon. Treasurer Dave McMillan and Paula Lively were wed in the Oxfordshire village of Marston on 24th June. Bruce Barton and Amanda married in Shepperton on 15th July and honeymooned in the USA and the Bahamas, while Nikki Haines, key member of our medal winning London Marathon squad last Spring, married Graeme Neal in Alderney on 27th December. George Flanagan married Anne. Our very best wishes go to all of them.

Tony Stone recently had a fall while running on the Common, resulting in a broken bone in his foot and a four day spell in hospital. He is now hobbling about on crutches but it didn't stop him from getting up to Belgrave Hall on the day of our home men's Surrey League race. He now has a plate in his foot but no doubt we'll see him back jogging before too long.

Also in the wars a little while back was Dennis Jones. At Wimbledon Station while heading for the Hall he was badly bitten on the hand by a dog. While medical assistance was called for Dennis was quite adamant: "It wasn't the dog's fault. I startled it".

We are sorry to report that our old comrade Dave McMullen is now incapacitated and in a nursing home. For those of his club-mates who would like to write to Dave, or maybe call in and see him, the address is [address withheld].

John Powell is not one to stay idle. Having retired as Team Manager of our most successful ever Junior T&F team he has taken on posts as Secretary to the AAA of England (T&F) and British and English Junior Indoor Team Manager. He continues to provided administrative backup to our Young Athletes' Section and is now putting the finishing touches to our Child Protection Policy. And of course he continues to coach some of the best young sprinters in the country.

Derek Jones celebrates 25 years of association with our Women's Southern League this summer. During that time he has been Women's Secretary, Team Manager, Coach, Field Event Judge and is currently the Division Three Administrator. Well done Derek.

There is plenty of talent in the Lambert family. Not only have the Lambert boys featured in all of our young athletes' teams with father Jon as Team Manager, but there is artistic talent as well. 18 year-old Alex has designed an eye-catching poster and brochure which is being used in schools across South London in our 2001 recruitment drive.

Visiting Lichfield en route to the National 6 stage relay one of our avid collectors of athletic memorabilia found a secondhand bookshop and bought a programme for the Birchfield Harriers Diamond Jubilee meeting, incorporating the Waddilove meeting, dated 24 July 1937. Belgrave entries for that meeting included E Gray (100 yds) T D Wiley (100 yds) reserves F W Pritchard and S C Botwright. 440 yds D G Michael, H E Pack, reserves T H Scrimshaw & A A Bird. 880 yds L W Herbert, A A Bird reserves T H Scrimshaw, W E Lucas, F H C White. 2 Mile Scratch team L J Cohen, J Parker, A W Penny, C T Carter, D Patience, J Ginty, E S Stimpson, H W Shields. 1 Mile Relay inter Club (880+440+220+220): E Gray, T D Wiley, S C Botwright, F W Pritchard, H E Pack, R G Michael, T H Scrimshaw, A A Bird, L W Herbert, F H C White, F R Webb, W E Lucas.

According to the Belgravian (August 1937) "we sent a team to Birmingham to compete in the Waddilove Trophy track competition for the first time. Birchfield Harriers have been anxious for years that we should participate, and at our first attempt we were glad to become joint holders, with the London A C of the handsome trophy. We were successful in winning the Two Mile team race through the efforts of A W Penny, L J Cohen and E S Stimpson; and took second place in the Relay (won by Birchfield), our team being A A Bird, R G Michael, S C Botwright and E Gray. In addition Bird ran wonderfully well to finish second to J V Powell, beaten by a yard and a half in 1 min. 57 3/5 seconds. E Gray was placed third in the 100 yds.

Final scores were Belgrave H and L A C 12 points each; Birchfield H., 10 pts; the holders, Achilles, scoring 1 point only.

All our runners did very well, and we returned feeling well satisfied with or first visit to Perry Bar."

Feature · page 13

How an Englishman influenced the birth of the Modern Olympic Games

EWH

An Olympic Story

How an Englishman influenced the birth of the Modern Olympic Games

This year all thoughts were on Sydney the venue for the Olympic Games but we wonder how many have an idea of where and how this fairly modern reintroduction of a Greek concept came about. Ask most people with an interest in sport about the origins and you might hear about Athens, Dorando Pietri, perhaps Baron de Coubertin but you would be unlikely to hear about Much Wenlock.

A sleepy little town in Shropshire not far from Ironbridge, the birthplace of the Industrial Revolution, seems a far cry from anything to do with the movement based in Lausanne, Switzerland. There are other places with a claim to fame as having held "Olympic" type games down the years but none have as much right to claim to be the forerunners of the series of events which culminated in the celebrations in Sydney in September. If you doubt the truth of this you have only to visit the town and walk the Olympic Trail to the Linden Fields where you will find trees to celebrate the Baron's visit in 1890. The reason for the visit was his anxiety to seek out Doctor William Penny Brookes who had been instrumental in founding the Wenlock Olympian Class in 1850. The objective was to "improve the moral, physical and intellectual improvement of the Town and neighbourhood of Wenlock".

The first Games, held in 1850, were a mixture of athletics and traditional country sports, including fun games. Some events were open to all comers and competitors travelled from a wide area including London. Within a few years Penny Brookes was in contact with Greece and sent £10 to the winner of an event in the Olympian Games in Athens in 1859. These Games survived in various forms for a few years and Penny Brookes kept in touch with the Greeks and to mark Queen Victoria's jubilee in 1887 the Greek King forwarded an inscribed cup for presentation at the National Olympian Games in Shrewsbury. This brought about a meeting between Penny Brookes and the Greek Charge d'Affaire in London which was cultivated with the hope that some form of International Festival, to be held in Athens, would come about. Unfortunately the Greek government of the day declined.

Brookes was not downcast and set about writing to people such as Gladstone, the Prime Minister, and other notables, to advise on the need for physical education in schools - sport for all you might say. In 1889 Baron Coubertin, organiser of an International Congress on Physical Education, appealed for information through the English newspapers. Brookes responded and so impressed the Baron that the latter travelled to Wenlock in 1890. A meeting was arranged in his honour and the young Baron, then aged 27, referred to his host's efforts to revive the Olympics in an article in "'La Revue Athletique," "If the Olympic Games that Modern Greece has not been able to revive still survives today, it is due, not to a Greek, but to Dr W P Brookes".

Unfortunately Brookes died in 1895 just 4 months before the Athens Games of 1896 were held. The Wenlock sports survived in some form or other, apart from during the two world wars, and this year celebrated the 150th anniversary. In the intervening years they have celebrated and honoured particular events and people. In 1980 a special festival was held to mark the centenary of the founding of the AAAs in 1880; in 1986 its own 100th Games were attended by members of the BOA and the grand nephew of Baron Coubertin. In 1990 the Guest of Honour was the President of the BOA, H.R.H. The Princess Royal. Four years later the President of the I.O.C. Juan Samaranch attended. In 1995 the Olympic Flame rested in the town overnight en route to Bath for the opening of the European Youth Olympics.

There is also a close association between The Olympians, an association open to all competitors in the Olympic Games, who held a meeting in the town in 1995 and returned again this year to mark the 150th anniversary of the birth of Penny Brookes' infant.

If you are ever in that part of the country make time to call in to see the memorabilia housed in the Museum and Guildhall as well as the lovely old buildings in a picturesque town which hides its light under a bushel to some extent.

EWH

William Penny BrookesPenny BrookesBrookesW P BrookesDorando PietriBaron de CoubertinBaron CoubertinQueen VictoriaGladstoneH.R.H. The Princess RoyalJuan SamaranchEWH

Report · page 14

Bels. bid for Olympic places

AAA Championships & Olympic Trials

Bels. bid for Olympic places

Birmingham, 11-13 August 2000

The 100 metres clash between Belgrave sprint titans Darren Campbell (European Champion) and Dwain Chambers (World Championships Bronze Medallist) with 17 year-old Mark Lewis Francis (Birchfield) and Jason Gardner (Bath) also vying for the top slot, fully lived up to expectations. Campbell looked so smooth in his semi-final but Chambers ran an powerful 10.28 in spite of audaciously slowing down over the final 20 metres; his poor form of earlier in the year seemed to have been put well behind him. Come the final and we had the magnificent sight of the two Belgrave men racing stride for stride but with Chambers just those few inches ahead. He was delighted to take his first AAA title and proved yet again that he is a fine Championship runner.

Darren Campbell overcame his disappointment to bounce back in the 200 metres, a clear winner, while Chambers decided not to risk turning out in the final after a worrying twinge at the end of his semi had him limping off the track urgently in search of ice. One was left hoping that his problem was only short-lived.

One of the biggest break-throughs of the Championships came from the purple-haired Philips Idowu who triple jumped 16.87 to beat rival Larry Achike and once again break the Olympic Qualifying standard. Idowu was quoted as saying, "I am absolutely delighted that I'm going to the Olympics. I really didn't think I was going to win."

Mike Jones retained his Hammer title after establishing an early lead but the big man from Sussex was still left searching for that elusive OQ standard that would see him included in the team.

Cardiff's Jamie Baulch got the shock of his life in the 400 metre semi-final when Sean Baldock rocketed past him coming into the home straight. Baulch responded to clock the fastest time by a European this year - 45.06 - to win, but Sean's 45.20 was a big pb and an OQ time into the bargain. Could he reproduce it in the final? Well, he couldn't go quite so fast but again he was under the standard and this time had the beating of Baulch to clinch his team place with a fine 2nd position to Mark Richardson. The Belgrave Harrier looked totally in shock afterwards: "I still cannot believe it. Last month in Budapest I ran the worst race of my life. I then came here and learned to run the 400m properly."

Ben Challenger claimed his first ever AAA title, beating his club-mate Brendan Reilly on count back after they both reached 2.22m. They both tackled the OQ of 2.28 with Ben coming very close at his second attempt.

A clear favourite, Chris Rawlinson was in no danger of being beaten in the 400 metre hurdles and his impressive 48.95 in the poor conditions showed that he was in the form to dip under 48 in the right race. Matt Douglas ran close to his best ever in 3rd place.

Steeplechaser Justin Chaston took up the early running in his speciality event and was on course for the OQ mark for half the race. He had to give way to Cardiff's Stephenson but, again, both of them were left looking a fast race to earn their Olympic places.

100m: (-0.8) 1, D.Chambers 10.11 (h5 1 10.28/0.7, sf2 1 10.28/0.0); 2, D.Campbell 10.12 (h2 1 10.31/0.2, sf1 1 10.34/0.5); hts h6 3, M.Dickson 10.72/0.5. 200m: (-1.2) 1, D.Campbell 20.49 (h1 1 20.82/1.8, sf1 2 20.71/-0.8); sf2 2, D.Chambers 20.87/-1.9 (h4 1 20.78/-1.5). 400m: 2, S.Baldock 45.71 (h3 1 46.19, sf2 2 45.20); hts h2 4, M.Parper 48.12. 800m: sf2 6, J.Lobo 1:52.13 (h1 2 1:50.21); h3 6, C.Gilby 1:54.21. 5000m: dnf Paul Evans; S.Barden. 110mH: (-2.3) 5, N.Owen 14.33 (h2 2 14.24/-0.6). 400mH: 1, C.Rawlinson 48.95 (h3 1 50.54); 3, M.Douglas 49.89 (h2 1 50.46); 8, N.Levy 52.76 (h1 2 51.80). 3000mSC: 2, J.Chaston 8:32.21 (h1 1 8:41.97); hts h2 10, L.Hurst 9:12.69. Report · page 14

MIKE JONES IN TERRIFIC FORM - BUT TOO LATE !

At the Crawley Open Meet on the evening of 6th September Mike launched his hammer out to 75.94 to exceed the Olympic Qualifying distance by 44 centimetres and increase his Club and Personal Record by 96cm. Entries for the Olympic Games needed to be in by that very same day but the result card was faxed to the relevant authorities and we were left to wait see if another Belgravian would be added to the British Team.

Sadly, no! The deadline for earning selection by achieving the Olympic 'A' standard, it transpired, was actually August 20th and although other athletes had been added to the list after that date, they had already been named in the squad as relay runners.

So Britain had no Hammer thrower at the Games.

Mike was very disappointed of course but immediately set himself a new target - 77.65m to beat the 2001 IAAF World Championships qualifying distance - and that's further than the British Record!

Mike JonesMike
See it on the printed page →
Report · page 14

CHASTON CLINCHES IT

At a BMC Meeting at Solihull on 19th August Britain's steeplechasers made another attempt to earn Olympic selection by cracking the OQ standard of 8:27.

A week earlier at the AAA Championships Justin Chaston and Cardiff's Christian Stephenson had planned to run this sort of time but came unstuck due to the blustery weather. Justin, based in Houston, Texas, was supposed to have returned to work in the USA during the week but the lure of one more attempt kept him in the UK and this time the conditions were good.

Kanda of Kenya led for the first kilometre before Stephenson took over with Chaston right on his shoulder - positions they maintained for the rest of the race. The bell time was 7:23 so the required finish time was "on" but it was going to be so close. Still together they maintained the pace around the final circuit - no mishaps at the final barrier - and finally Stephenson was across the finish line with the race clock stopping on 8:25.37. So, the Cardiff man had done it but had the Belgravian finished close enough to him. A few anxious moments ticked past before it was agreed that he had indeed earned himself a place on the team - 8:26.07.

Justin ChastonJustinChastonChristian StephensonStephensonKanda
