Report · page 16

Belgrave Olympians shine in Sydney

Sydney 2000

Belgrave Olympians shine in Sydney

Chambers and Campbell 4th and 6th in Olympic 100 metres final

100 metres: Rounds 1 and 2 - 22nd Sep., Semi-finals and Final - 23rd Sep. The initial round of the 100 metres saw Belgravians Dwain Chambers and Darren Campbell both comfortably progressing to the next stage but drama struck for the second round when Chambers found himself suffering from cramp as the starter called the runners to their marks. A false start by another competitor didn't help and the powerful Belgrave man sank to the track with his face contorted as he urgently tried to rub his knotted calf muscles back to life. Somehow he lined up again and when the gun fired, a pattering stride instead of his normal ground devouring gait kept him competitive without the spasms returning. Amazingly he won his race in 10.12 before veering straight off the track and into the physio's room. Darren Campbell had no trouble in the following race, winning in 10.21.

Next day both the Belgrave boys came through the semi-final stage with flying colours. If Chambers' legs were troubling him it didn't show now as he won his race in 10.14 with Campbell 3rd in 10.19. Almost inseparable in so many races this summer - it stayed that way to the end as both now lined up for the Olympic final.

Both had good starts but if there were ever any doubts about the World Record holder Maurice Greene, they were all swept away by 50 metres when the American was clear. Trinidadian Ato Boldon looked looked pretty safe for second but Chambers and Campbell were in the thick of the action with 20 metres remaining, the former just about in the bronze medal position. That finish line just wouldn't come quick enough though. With tensed up shoulders Chambers dipped a shade too early and instead of snatching a medal, the chance was lost as Obadale Thompson slipped past to book his place on the rostrum.

Round 1 h5 (-0.5) 1, D.Campbell 10.28; h7 (0.3) 2, D.Chambers 10.38. Round 2 h4 (0.8) 1, D.Chambers 10.12; h5 (0.2) 1, D.Campbell 10.21. Semi-finals sf1 (0.4) 1, D.Chambers 10.14; 3, D.Campbell 10.19. Final (-0.3) 1, M.Greene (USA) 9.88; 2, A.Boldon (Trinidad & Tobago) 9.99; 3, O.Thompson (Barbados) 10.04; 4, D.Chambers (GB & NI) 10.08; 5, J.Drummond (USA) 10.09; 6, D.Campbell (GB & NI) 10.13; 7, K.Collins (St. Kitts & Nevis) 10.17; dnf, A.Zakari (Ghana).

Darren Campbell - Olympic Silver and third fastest Briton of all time

200 metres: Rounds 1 and 2 - 27th Sep., Semi-final and Final - 28th Sep. Any disappointment that Darren Campbell felt after his 6th place in the 100 metres final just seemed to add fuel to his determination to shine in the 200 metres event. A 20.71 run in the opening round behind the USA's Coby Miller was followed a couple of hours later by a scintillating 20.13 to win his round two race. In spite of easing down over the final 35 metres and turning his head to keep an eye out for any fast finishers, he removed a huge chunk from his previous best (20.48) and beat Ato Bolden to boot. Among Britons only Regis and Christie have gone faster.

In his semi-final Darren led down into home straight but eased off again as the finish was approached, allowing the Greek Kederis to claim the win - 20.20 to 20.23. With Cardiff's Christian Malcolm successfully handling his very tough semi, there were two Britons in the final again.

Drawn in lane 6 with Obadale Thompson (Barbados) and Ato Boldon (Trinidad & Tobago) occupying the tracks outside him, Darren started superbly, ran a magnificent bend and entered the finish straight half a metre up on the two favourites to his right and a full metre clear of the rest of the field. Staying relaxed he held everything together but the man from Greece was running a storming final 50 metres. Kederis came from the middle of the field, into second, and finally overtook the Belgrave Harrier with only a few metres remaining.

Darren was so close to gold that for an instant second place was a disappointment - but for goodness sake - he'd beaten all the pre-race favourites, confirmed his position as no. 1 Briton and had been run down by an inspired Konstadinos Kederis. Holder of the Greek national 400 metres record, no wonder Kederis was so strong at the end; it was his country's first men's track and field medal since 1912. Darren's Silver Medal was the first individual Olympic medal by a Belgravian since Stan Vickers' 20 kms bronze in Rome, 1960.

Round 1 h4 (0.2) 2, D.Campbell 20.71. Round 2 h1 (0.3) 1, D.Campbell 20.13. Semi-finals sf2 (0.3) 2, D.Campbell 20.23. Final (-0.6) 1, K.Kederis (Greece) 20.13; 2, D.Campbell (GB & NI) 20.14; 3, A.Boldon (Trinidad & Tobago) 20.20; 4, O.Thompson (Barbados) 20.20; 5, C.Malcolm (GB & NI) 20.23; 6, C. da Silva (Brasil) 20.28; 7, C.Miller (USA) 20.35; 8, J.Capel (USA) 20.49.

Sean shocked in the long sprint

400 metres: Round 1 - 22nd Sep. A recent 400 metres personal record of 45.20 by Sean Baldock gave him and his supporters every hope that he would get to the semi-final stage of the one lap sprint; but athletics doesn't always go the way we want it to. Racing on the lane just inside World Record holder Michael Johnson, Sean found himself up on the maestro's shoulder at the half-way point - and there it all went wrong. Whereas Johnson was cruising, the Belgrave man had already pushed the pedal to the floor without realising it. Coming out of the turn he had nothing left at all and as runners streamed past him it was all he could do to keep moving towards the finish line.

Round 1 h7 5, S.Baldock 46.45.

Douglas and Rawlinson reach the semi-finals

400 metres hurdles: Round 1 - 24th Sep., Semi-finals - 25th Sep. Matt Douglas and Chris Rawlinson successfully moved through the heats of the 400 metres hurdles in spite of the howling gale against them in the back straight. Rawlinson won his race in 51.30 - over 2 seconds slower than his best but Douglas was a mere 3/10ths down on his best mark as he ran home 3rd in 49.62 in the fastest heat of all, won by Zambia's Matete in 48.98.

Sadly neither of our boys progressed beyond the semi-finals. If he had been able to reproduce his very best form Chris would have gone into the final but as it was, he was reduced to 6th place in 49.25. For Matt Douglas it was back to his favourite lane (!) - the outside one - for his semi-final. He ran a storming 300 metres but he too was run down in the straight to finish 6th in 49.53. On the world rankings list Matt was outside the top 120 in 1999 with a best ever of 50.20; he's come a long, long way in 2000.

Round 1 h5 1, C.Rawlinson 51.30. h7 3, M.Douglas 49.62. Semi-finals sf2 6, C.Rawlinson 49.25; sf3 6, M.Douglas 49.53.

One "barrier" too many for Chaston

3000 metres Steeplechase: Round 1 - 27th Sep. Justin Chaston found that he had to hurdle one "barrier" too many when a Mexican competitor fell in front of him as they came off one of the hurdles but it didn't really upset his rhythm and the US based Belgravian ran to his second best time of the season - 8:31.01 - to place 7th in his heat. A solid enough run but unfortunately not enough to take him into the next round.

Round 1 h3 6, J.Chaston 8:31.01.

Ben and Bren unable to reach final

High Jump: Qualifying round - 22nd Sep. Ben Challenger and Brendan Reilly were unable to get into qualifying mode, Ben in particular looked only a shadow of the jumper who cleared 2.30 metres twice last year to win the World University Champs. and European Under 23 Champs. He placed equal 13th in his pool with a first time clearance at 2.15 before three failures at 2.20. In the other pool Brendan entered the competition at 2.20, which he cleared at his second attempt, before finding 2.24, his outdoor season's best, beyond him on this occasion. They both would have needed 2.27 to progress to the final.

Qual. Group A =13, B.Challenger 2.15 (2.15o, 2.20xxx); Group B 11, B.Reilly (IRL) 2.20 (2.20xo, 2.24xxx).

Idowu now among the World's finest

Triple Jump: Qualifying round - 23rd Sep., Final - 25th Sep. The flamboyant Phillips Idowu, sporting claret coloured hair, took just one attempt in the Triple Jump qualifying round - sailing out to 17.12 metres for a personal and Club record. It was the second longest leap from all of the competitors. Into the final and no messin'.

Two days later a 16.97 leap gave Phillips the 5th best performance in the opening round of the final with the two other Britons ahead of him - Larry Achike leading at 17.29 and Jonathon Edwards 4th with 17.12. The Russian Kapustin took the lead with a 17.46 effort in round 2 while Phillips fouled and Edwards moved up to 2nd with 17.37. Round 3 and Jonathon Edwards wrapped up the competition by leaping out to 17.71 for the year's World leading distance and, eventually, the gold medal. By this time the Belgrave man had been pushed down to 7th but another fine jump over the 17 metre mark in the fourth round moved him into 6th place which he maintained to the end of the competition. Phillips now stands at no. 7 on the British all-time list.

Qual. Group B 1, P.Idowu 17.12 (17.12/-0.6). Final 1, J.Edwards (GB & NI) 17.71; 2, Y.Garcia (Cuba) 17.47; 3, D.Kapustin (Russia) 17.46; 4, Y.Quesada (Cuba) 17.37; 5, L.Achike (GB & NI) 17.29; 6, P.Idowu (GB & NI) 17.08 (16.97/1.3, x, 16.83/0.0, 17.08/0.0, x, x).

Nick Sweeney below par

Discus: Qualifying round - 24th Sep. Nick holds our club best at 67.89 but although he hasn't been near that distance since 1998, pretty good marks, including a 61.76, came from him in the days before the Games. But the Irish record holder was unable to get near this in Stadium Australia and ended up a long way short of qualifying.

Qual. Group B 18. N.Sweeney (IRL) 57.37 (56.73, 56.24, 57.37)

Letter · page 18

Aussie, Aussie, Aussie

Clive "Alistair" Shippen

Letter from Australia by Clive "Alistair" Shippen

Aussie, Aussie, Aussie

Oi! Oi! Oi! Yes, the chant is still ringing in my ears. It greeted me everywhere; in the supermarket, the Post-Office, the bottle shop, and especially in the streets.

The Australians love a party and just don't want to go home, so the Olympics were stretched a week at each end to become a 4-week jaunt of high spirits. Nothing much happened outside of the Olympics. The cynics who couldn't get out of town quick enough before the Games are now beginning to realise just what they missed. There were no disasters, no terrorists, no violence, no racism, no drunkenness to speak of (amazing) - just lots of fun, happiness, laughter and friendliness to all.

During the Games themselves the tone was set by the thousands of casually uniformed volunteers. Where did they find so much good humour? And so many comedians? They were everywhere. It was impossible to stand anywhere in the gigantic Olympic Park without seing one. At one key junction a beach life-guard, daubed in sun-screen and perched on the top of his high step-ladder, advised the crowd to swim between the flags "but remember - no nudity is allowed!". Other volunteers megaphoned their advice and news in verse, which produced much banter from the crowd who were not exactly short of comedians themselves. Everyone was made to feel welcome.

If ever an occasion proved what could be done with public transport this was it. With about 300,000 spectator movements per day, 95% were by bus or train. Special buses (no fare) ran every two minutes from my suburb and I never waited for more than one minute. Olympic Clearway lanes took me to the Park in a fast 30 minutes. On arrival there was a security check by the friendliest of personnel, with no waiting.

Olympic Park is vast, unrecognisable from four years ago. The principal thoroughfare, Olympic Boulevard, cuts it's way through a never-ending array of sports buildings. At the southern end lies the tennis centre with it's 10,000 seater centre court, a No.1 and No.2 court, and 13 outer courts; while at the northern end across the water - yes, there is water everywhere between Homebush and the sea - lies the athletes' village which is destined to become a permanent suburb.

What of the competition? Well, I decided to see both the women's and men's triathlons first. What a sport that is; a 1500 metres swim in the Harbour followed by a 40km cycle ride over a hilly course and then a 10,000 metres run over the same lap. Both days were hot and the runners suffered. Lessing (GBR), who has run about 14:30 for 5000m this year, was reckoned to be a medallist, but after leading the cycle race at halfway faded, and faded again during the run to finish 10th. The crowds cheered their favourites of course but there was much support for the other competitors, particularly the British.

This was something I noticed in the main stadium. One day I sat in the stand with three real, real Aussies next to me and another three similar types behind me. It was noisy! But it was good humoured and quite funny at times. At regular intervals all three of my neighbours would depart for ten minutes and return with replenished glasses of Fosters. I began to feel uneasy - a Pom surrounded by inebriated colonials is not an ideal scenario. I concentrated on the Decathlon competition and cheered Macey at every opportunity, without thinking. After a time the fellow next to me joined in, then the one behind, until all six were cheering Macey vociferously. We all groaned together when he just missed the bronze medal and for the tenth time I drew my legs in to allow my neighbours to pass for their final exit. In slurring voices they bade me goodbye like a close friend "See you later", but I don't suppose I ever shall.

The beer flowed; the wine too (Lindemanns had a huge marquee devoted to their wines) yet I saw no cases of drunkenness, violence or even bad behaviour. Good humour and friendliness prevailed throughout - everyone seemed so happy. Sydneysiders are now suffering withdrawal symptoms!

I thought I'd sample some handball, a sport I'd never seen live. It was held in the Dome (not the Superdome which is about four times bigger). It's a game rather like water-polo without the water. The women's quarter-finals were being contested. It was worth going just to see the Korean supporters! The Aussies had been eliminated but the crowd soon adopted a new favourite. Australians simply love any sporting contest

I won't go into the highs and lows of the athletics performances; everyone knows what happened. All I would say is that no one screamed Darren Campbell to the line louder than I did; willed him past Ato Bolden and the two Americans, only to see a Greek come from nowhere and pip him for that covetted gold medal. I ask you, a Greek? Shouldn't be allowed. A great run Darren.

And so to the last day. Olympic fever had gripped me during that last week so I also visited the stadium for the football final, prior to the evening's athletics. It was Spain versus Cameroon. I don't think the Aussies knew quite what to expect here. After all, 100,000 at a European match would be something of a tinder box. So the Aussies dealt with the situation by placing single chairs around the track at 50 metre intervals on which sat policemen facing the crowd. Think of it you Chelsea supporters - 8 coppers for 100,000 spectators! It was the most I'd seen during the whole Games. Of course, nothing happened.

The crowd backed Cameroon hugely. They didn't like the antics of Spaniards when felled and let them know it in very plain language. Two swarthy South Africans took their seats alongside me ten minutes after the start. "Have we missed much?" they asked. "Not really," I replied, "Spain have scored a goal and missed a penalty". They decided that was another good reason to support Cameroon and laid in to any writhing Spaniard. It was soon, knees to one side to allow the Foster's beakers to be replenished. The frequency of these visits caused them to miss another goal, but to their delight two Spaniards were sent off and the match went to extra time. A penalty shoot-out gave victory to Cameroon 5-4 but I don't know why that made everyone so happy. The Spanish supporters sportingly waved their flags and cheered when the victors received their gold medals, but I don't know exactly what they were saying!

After the medal ceremony the stadium was emptied of it's 100,000 football enthusiasts, cleaned, and then re-opened in one and a half hours for 110,000 athletics fans to witness the last session of the Games. We all know what happened on relay day, and how strange it was to see no 4x100 British team and a very tame 4x400 squad. But the crowd roared just the same - deafeningly.

And so to Sunday, the men's marathon and the closing ceremony. We staked out a patch about 30 metres from the north embankment of the Harbour Bridge, opposite the Opera House, by the water's edge. Several hundred were already there at 3.30pm to prepare for the night's fireworks. We nipped up onto the bridge approaches to see Botswana lead the marathon field shortly after the start and then returned to our site to see the race played out on the huge TV screen erected to entertain the waiting crowds. There were several of these throughout the City during the Games at designated celebration areas.

The tempo of revelry grew during the afternoon as the crowds steadily thickened. Thirsty hands dipped frequently into "Eskies" of wine and beer, and the cheers for Moneghetti became more animated. But Jon Brown's excellent effort to hold the African marathoners for so long got much support from those around us.

Hours later the closing ceremony was well underway and the crowd became even more lively. A middle-aged couple nearby, clad in out of date Polish track suits, proudly showed their origins but they were real Aussies all the same. I was reminded of those supporters in the stadium who had been waving Australian flags for much of the evening, only for Greek flags to appear when Kenteris beat Darren in the 200 metres, after which Australian flags resumed their waving. The tempo of dancing and singing increased as the closing ceremony reached it's climax and excitement grew as the fireworks became due.

And then they came. An electric storm of noise and colour headed towards us down the Parramatta river. When it struck the Harbour it triggered a huge display of fireworks from water barges, tops of buildings and the Bridge itself. It took a further ten minutes to reach it's climax and when it did so we found ourselves inside a waterfall of sound and light - totally engulfed. Almost frightening in it's splendour.

Gradually everthing quietened down and the crowds made their way home peacefully in the early hours without incident.

Was it all over at last? Not on your life. The Australians don't let go that easily. The following day was declared a public holiday for starters. The next day the City streets were closed as Sydney gave a ticker-tape welcome to the whole Olympic team - who then had to repeat the exercise in the remaining five states to ensure that no one was left out. And then the next day the streets were closed again as a >>>

>>> ticker-tape "thank you" welcome was held for the Olympic volunteers, and I must say they deserved it.

So Sydney 2000 has drawn to a close. The City and State government have good cause to be proud of their effort. I doubt if there is another city in the world that is blessed with the facilities to host every Olympic sport within it's boundaries (excepting a few preliminary football matches). This humble Pom feels privileged to have been present. Just as the Australians cheered on the Poms after the Aussies, I did the reverse. But I think of those Poles and Greeks I met and wonder how long it will be before I too will be carrying two flags. Either way I shall always be a Belgrave Harrier.

Now for the Paralympics - Phew! Here we go - Aussie, Aussie, Aussie - Oi! Oi! Oi!

Clive ShippenLessingMaceyDarren CampbellDarrenAto BoldenMoneghettiJon BrownKenteris

Column · page 19

From The Belgravian

25 Years ago - 1975

An editorial contained the then startling news that "Frank Shorter, the US Olympic marathon champion, a lawyer by profession, - publicly admitting that he has received £100 per month while touring Europe". This, it was said, put the IAAF in a difficult position! Of equal importance was a move towards having a ladies section. The intervening 25 years have seen significant developments on both fronts.

Some things, however, never change and we had Gordon Biscoe quoting Shakespeare to encourage the Road and Country section and Bill Couzens, who took over from Ray Hall as President, was noted as supporting Fulham FC and as host of rowdy parties!

We staged the AAA 6 Stage Relay from the Hall but last minute injuries meant that a weakened team came home 24th of the 31 starters.

Fifty years ago - 1950

To start with an apology for a mistake in the last issue - The walkers were led home over the shorter distances by Harry Churcher rather than Harry Shields who had, at that time not even taken up the gentler sport of race walking!

An article on Coaching, by Chief Coach Charlie Jones, noted that we had exclusive use of the Duke of York's Barracks, Chelsea, and that plans had been made to develop specialised sessions on Wednesday evenings. Numbers were restricted and nominations by coaches had to be made to permit entry. (Little did we know how honoured we were to receive advice from Franz Stampfl, Frank Simmons, Bert Footer, Frank Elson and others wearing their Club track suits with coloured flashes!) Fifty years on and the Duke of York's is due for demolition and redevelopment.

As was the custom in those days tickets for the AAA's Championships were sold through the Club and everyone was urged to apply early to avoid disappointment.

Sixty years ago - 1940

The War had an effect on Club activities but there was a desire to carry on despite difficulties. Battersea Park was closed and we moved to Tooting Bec and members were advised to read "The Amateur Athlete", an Armour Milne weekly publication, to keep abreast of matters.

The second Belgravian of the year noted the deaths of several members killed in action or in air raids plus a list of 85 members in the services and a futher 4 with the fire service, police and merchant navy. Even then acknowledgement is made that there were others of whom the details were not known. Allowing for all this disruption inter club competition was still possible and among notable marks were a 4m 35.4 mile by Bill Lucas against TCC a combination of members from Blackheath, London AC and South London. The "News of the World" relay was held at Mitcham and consisted of a lap of the track followed by a mile on the road for each runner. The report notes that Les Cohen was able "to hand over a long lead to Charlie Smart, who continued after the restart through an air raid warning in which the race was held up". This gave a whole new meaning to interval training!

Frank ShorterGordon BiscoeBill CouzensRay HallHarry ChurcherHarry ShieldsCharlie JonesFranz StampflFrank SimmonsBert FooterFrank ElsonArmour MilneBill LucasLes CohenCharlie Smart

Report · page 20

COUPE D'EUROPE des CLUBS CHAMPIONS BELGRAVE TRIUMPHANT!

Eric Hall

Track & Field - European Champion Clubs Group B

COUPE D'EUROPE des CLUBS CHAMPIONS BELGRAVE TRIUMPHANT!

Report by Eric Hall

Liege, Belgium - 27/28 May 2000 The venue for the Group B competition - Liege, Belgium - meant a flight from Heathrow to Brussels followed by the first of a series of very interesting, tortuous and misguided coach tours of Belgium during which we saw every Avenue in Liege at least twice! Our contact at Brussels Airport told us he never had problems only solutions and that his method for spotting athletic teams was infallible - he waited until someone carrying poles arrived and voila there was the team! Not so Belgrave as the airline had sent Ben Flint's poles somewhere other than Brussels!

Having taken a very long time getting to Liege we made up for it on the way to the hotel only to find a hotel it was not! A youth hostel, ten miles out in the country, sans towels and soap was the best they could find for us and the meal made available did not bode too well. However an early night and everyone was ready for the competition against teams from France, Israel, Slovakia, Holland and Cyprus. The programme gave details of each athlete's best performance and quick calculations showed that it would be a close run thing between ourselves and CA Montreuil 93 representing France. Every point would count and the very blustery conditions concentrated the mind somewhat.

The stadium had been recently refurbished and this meant a series of welcoming speeches and presentations followed by a buffet lunch for the nominated officials (in our case Mavis and I kept pace with the opposition with regard to the wine if not the eats!). The teams paraded followed by more speeches and then the meeting began. Matt Douglas set the required standard in winning the 400 metres hurdles and Darren Campbell matched this in the 100 metres. Among the next results, the discus found Kevin Brown in 2nd which was better than expected and Phillips Idowu was only 16 centimetres short of his best in winning the triple jump while the Frenchman fell short of his by some way; we were on song.

The 1500 metres found the smallest runner, Belgrave team mascot Yacin Yusuf, up against some stronger looking competitors but did well to come home a very welcome 2nd behind Samir of France a 3m 35s runner. Another slight figure, Lee Hurst, then took centre stage for the steeplechase which quickly developed into two races, the first three then an ever widening gap to the next trio. Lee stuck it out very well and came home 3rd but with a French runner heading the field it was getting closer. Brendan Reilly then set about putting us further ahead by jumping 2.16 metres while Detchenique of Montreuil was well below his best and finished 4th. Following this example Sean Baldock murdered the 400m field by over a second. The 4 x 100 relay squad had a rough time but to everyone's relief finished 2nd to Montreuil. The final event of the day, the pole vault, was held in the worst possible conditions with everyone affected by the swirling, blustery, wind. Ben Flint had spent the night worrying about where his poles were and they were only delivered to the stadium during the afternoon - not ideal preparation. His attempts to enter the competition at 5 metres were unsuccessful and although he cleared the height by some distance he came down on the bar on the vital jump. Despite this we still ended the day ahead by just 2 points.

Sunday's weather was even worse with higher winds and some rain about. Another tortuous coach ride and we arrived at the stadium with only minutes to spare despite leaving the hostel two hours before the start time! Nerves were already on edge without that type of encouragement. Mick Jones was the right man to calm the nerves and despite having, on paper, a poorer performance than the Montreuil thrower he beat him by some distance and took the stadium record for good measure. As the Slovakian athlete also beat the Frenchman we opened our lead. Neil Owen came home well clear in the 110 metres hurdles where the Frenchman was only 4th but with some field events to come we needed a cushion. Jason Lobo almost gave us heart attacks in the 800 but came away in the last few metres to win a tactical race. Now we had a 13 point advantage but no one was superstitious about that! Darren Campbell eased his way to the hoped for double in taking the 200 metres. Kevin Brown also doubled up but found the shot putt field very hot. The Slovakian winner, Konopka was near his best mark over 5 metres further than Kevin who took 5th place. A similar place was earned by Darren Thompson in the long jump where the freakish wind played a very important part. It varied from 3.3 metres in favour to 1.7 against! Despite a Montreuil win in the long jump and a second in the shot we were still 8 points ahead with three events to come. Stuart Faben finished 3rd in the javelin which was what we hoped for and in the 5,000 metres Spencer Barden was up against a Frenchman with a best time over 20 seconds faster than his. Spencer stuck to him in the early laps but the conditions were awful and although he held his form well, he tired, but was a well clear 2nd at the end leaving it all down to the 4 x 400 relay. Montreuil were clearly quite confident that this was their chance although the points tally showed that barring accidents the title was ours. Michael Parper led off and it was neck and neck when he handed over to Andrew Walcott who kept in touch, almost physically, with the Frenchman to hand on to Matt Douglas. Matt came home just behind to hand to Sean Baldock in the classic British relay tradition. Sean hung on, tackled his opponent at the bottom bend and then came strong in the final stages in the home straight to bring us home by 0.32 seconds and a 7 point win over the whole two days!

The trackside celebrations got totally out of hand when Yacin mistook which team he was with and sprayed the parading, victorious Belgrave athletes with water. This earned him a well deserved threat from Mick Jones to use him for his next hammer practice if he didn't desist! At the presentation dinner in the evening we found the organisers had used the decathlon scoring system to decide on champions at various disciplines. Highest performance, and running champion of the meeting, was Darren Campbell with Phillips Idowu taking the jump trophy for his triple jump. When it came to lifting the team trophy team captain, Darren Campbell, was slow out of his blocks and found most of the team on the temporary stage before he could get there. Fortunately no damage was done and the staging took the strain - just!!

A very emotional weekend full of memories but none will last longer than seeing the entire Belgrave team, and their few supporters, rise as one to applaud the French team when they were called to receive their award. This reflected the spirit in which the competition was held and, despite our early misgivings, we had a marvellous time with a great result. As John Jeffery wrote in my programme - "part of an amazing team, Best in Britain" and this was matched by Bill Laws "Management to the Aristocracy" Sentiments Mavis and I echo and we say "Thank You" to everyone who competed or supported the team.

To the team we say "Best of Luck in Group A next season!"

It was a comparatively uneventful run home although with only 20 minutes to spare to book in at Brussels airport the nail biting stayed with us to the end it was nice to see one word written on the top of almost the last building we saw from the air in Belgium - Belgravia! Then London was in view and we took in a number of tracks - Enfield, Battersea, the old Poly stadium, and others before touching down at Heathrow.

Report · page 22

BAL Team on top after unpredictable first match

Bill Laws

Track & Field - British Athletics League Division One

BAL Team on top after unpredictable first match

Reports and stats. by Bill Laws

Match 1 - Eton Stadium, 6 May 2000 With Olympic year making team availability uncertain for the later season league matches, we realised a good start would be essential if we were to continue our succession of division 1 wins.

The new track at Eton provided an entertaining and pleasant venue made even more interesting with Prince Harry competing in a college cricket match on a pitch adjacent to the stadium. The spectators spent the afternoon alternating between watching athletics and cricket. The weather was balmy but ruined for the sprinters by a headwind gusting up to 4m/sec.

As the afternoon unfolded, we were to realise yet again how unpredictable team athletics can be. The start seemed straight forward enough with Mick Jones winning the hammer with a 74m plus effort - some 5.5m ahead of second place Paul Head. Phil Spivey made a welcome return following a late withdrawal by Dave Smith. Little were we to know then that Dave would not make any of our matches during year 2000.

Matt Douglas opened the track events with a great win in the long hurdles clocking a sub 50 effort to confirm the rumours that his winter training had been going well. However, the excellent start was soon put into full reverse as first Richard Aspden withdrew from the high jump with a niggle and then Brendan Reilly (fresh from honeymoon) failed to beat 1.90. When did we last get only 5 points in the high jump? Soon after we scraped a further 8 points in the 800m only thanks to Birchfield having no runners in either the A or B event. Indeed, Birchfield had no-one in the steeplechase and pole vault either and with two last places in both relays slumped to 6th spot - their worst start for several decades.

But we weren't faring much better. With only Ian Tullett in the pole vault and no top line long jumper, the Belgrave management team was suddenly facing the prospect of a major defeat. Fortunately, as the afternoon unfolded, some highly respectable contributions from our throwers and sprinters stopped the rot. The final substantial margin of victory over Newham and Essex Beagles was however deceptive and was due in part to the fact that they had no one in the 5000m and a former team manager in the triple jump.

Special mention for Darren Campbell who supported the team at this early fixture. His participation made it possible for Marlon Dickson to pick up good points in the 'B' events. Matt Douglas, as usual, gave 110% support also winning the high hurdles and adding points in the long jump when things looked desperate.

Junior Scott Thompson made a welcome debut in the shot gaining a surprising 3rd spot in the 'A'; event. Stuart Faben pulled out one of his best ever early season throws in the javelin and Wesley Smith added further points in an event in which we have needed some depth. Wesley is young, keen and hopefully will push Stuart in the years ahead.

The final surprise for the day was saved for the last event. Tom Lerwill was spotted in the crowd spectating and was cajoled to take the third leg of the long relay. With Michael Parper running a storming last leg (following a great solo effort in the 'A' 400) the scratch squad of Richard David, Drew Walcott, Tom Lerwill and Michael won in a very surprising and highly respectable 3.11.33. Soon after Tom was to suffer further injury and his frustrations continue.

Report · page 22

Hurdlers prove to be big points earners

Bill Laws

Match 2 - Wigan, 24 June 2000 Normally, Match 2 of the BAL follows the week after the European Club Cup. So the postponement of the competition to the end of June (due to UK Athletics taking the traditional slot for its Road to Sydney TV series), was welcomed because of the venue - Wigan. We had hoped that being virtually on mid-summers day, good weather would be provided but it was not to be. Rain and winds up to 6m/sec were the order of the day and it was not surprising that only the really committed supported this one - which didn't make life easy for the team, spectators or management.

Our usual good start looked decidedly iffy as Mick Jones slipped around the waterlogged hammer circle. Going into the last round in an unusual second spot only Mick seemed confident that he could retain his 3 year unbeaten record. Oh ye of little faith! Our man saved his best for last. Not massive by his supreme standards but a fine win in dreadful conditions. Wesley Smith gained useful 'B' points and provided another name for our hammer rankings. In the javelin Wesley was our 'A' man and showed a useful improvement to 54m+ while Kevin Brown filled the 'B' slot. Kevin had beaten Lee Newman in a close discus competition but Lee gained good points in the shot while new man Dave Readle celebrated his first outing for Belgrave by winning the 'B' shot.

The hurdles were once again proving big points earners with Matt Douglas winning the 'A' event in terrible conditions by a 1.5 sec margin and Noel Levy making it maximum points with a 'B' win. Noel has chosen 2000 as his come back year after a long period on the sidelines. In the high hurdles, Neil Owen was blown to sub 14 sec effort while Noel Levy gained a fine second spot in the 'B' race for excellent points. The flat 400m provided welcome maximum points with Geoff Dearman posting a classy time in conditions that were far from perfect and Sean Baldock, who had had a useful blow out in the 200m, coasted round to win the 'B' race.

'Every point counts', enthuse the team managers at the start of every match. Well, on this occasion, the brave hearts who mixed and matched to fill places and earn points provided just enough to earn us a draw. Yes, a 500 mile round trip and we dead heated with home club Sale. But we were looking for Newham & Essex Beagles and they finished third just half a league point behind us. This was a better result than we could have hoped for at the start of the day but once again as we looked back on the results we realised how easily it could have gone either way.

Who, for example, would have predicted that Newham's Mark Proctor would elect to participate in his hobby event - the discus - and win with a 57m+ p.b. More worryingly, who would have predicted that our middle distance squad would perform so disappointingly. Admittedly Clive Gilby's hamstring pull in the 1500 could have caused him to pull out, so his brave hobble home saved the match but equally a fit Clive would have won. Additionally, the last place for our sole steeplechaser Steve Baldock, also proved crucial.

So there it was like a curates egg - good in parts. Well, excellent in parts really. With fine wins in the pole vault and excellent points also in the high and long jumps we really should have won this one. But we didn't. The final last ditch effort was typified by high jumper Richard Aspden legging it in the long relay. We've seen high jumpers proving proficient at the high hurdles but Richard, with his huge stride really looks the business when he's stretching out down the back straight. Has he missed his vocation?

Report · page 23

The Beagles "take us to the cleaners"

Match 3 - Copthall Barnet, 8 July 2000 Match 3 is where we try to move into an unassailable lead so that the opposition rolls over for the concluding Match 4. The Monday team looked quite respectable. By Wednesday the team was still looking adequate but by Friday, we were thumbing through old address books for the phone numbers of a few golden oldies. And bless them they responded to a man - all a bit creaky - but a response is a response and all the more welcome. So let's start by paying special tribute to; John Urquhart who can still throw a mean hammer; Matt Yates who could have scored good high jump points as he twanged his hamstring in the 800m but continued for 4 points; Jim Estall who coasted the 1500m in flats and had only come to watch Junior Galley in the steeplechase and last but no means least, Ian Wells who gained 10 valuable points in both hurdles plus the steeplechase.

But despite all these and many other valiant efforts we finished down in third spot behind Newham and Shaftesbury. In fact a strong Newham team took us to the cleaners, to close the league positions to just half a point, ensuring that the final Match would be a mighty struggle.

But back to Match 3. There are always several surprises at every competition, which is another good reason for spectators to support these matches. Perhaps the most interesting was the 'A' 400m hurdles with Duaine Ladejo in his Birchfield strip looking for an Olympic qualifying time. With Chris Rawlinson and Matt Douglas absent, Noel Levy moved into the 'A' slot brimming with confidence. We thought Noel was being a bit too confident so early into his comeback season, but not so. At the gun Duaine was away like a hare. 21 secs and into hurdle 5 he was 10m up on Noel, who was hurdling impeccably. But it looked a one horse race until the home straight when Duaine started treading water. The change was dramatic as Noel, still full of running, made huge inroads into what was once an impressive lead. The tape came too soon for our man who nonetheless returned an impressive 51.85.

The one lap flat race was no less exciting. We were missing Sean Baldock and Geoff Dearman from this one, so Michael Parper moved into the 'A' race again full of confidence. Quite rightly too, because in winning he beat some fine 400m men including Newham's David Naismith. His time of 47.08 was tantalisingly close to the sub 47 effort he was seeking. Andrew Walcott gained good points in the 'B' to ensure that once again the flat 400 would be one of our better events.

However, as usual our throwers provided the backbone to our total score. The hammer, discus, shot and javelin amassed 100 points out of a possible maximum 112, so not much to complain about there. Mick Jones retained his unbeaten record in the hammer, while Stuart Faben gained his first ever win in the 'A' javelin. Wesley Smith gained silver in the 'B' javelin improving yet again to 57.29m. Lee Newman pipped Kevin Brown in the discus but Dave Readle produced a magnificent 16.61m effort in the shot to surprisingly reverse the tables on Lee who is once again warming to this event.

The long jump proved disappointing, the triple jump partly disappointing, as Phillips Idowu put in one 15m plus effort and went home, while the high jump was very satisfactory as Samson Oni, seeking a further qualifying mark of 2.15m for the World Juniors, could only manage 2.10m for second spot on count back. Ian Tullett once again won the pole vault.

Sadly, the middle distance was once again disappointing. Paul Freary had recently returned from running a half marathon in riot torn Fiji and it showed. He was nearly caught by fellow Lancastrian Ric Hayman, who you may recall only started running this year and in this match reduced his 5000m time to 15.16. Ric is an ex-footballer and we wonder if he could be about to emulate our other former specialist with the round ball - Paul Evans.

The sprints were less productive than usual but special mention must go to Michael Parper, who not only won the 'A' 400m but also ran the 'B' 200m and both relays to complete a hard days work.

Report · page 24

Young tigers put result beyond doubt

Track & Field - British Athletics League Division One

Young tigers put result beyond doubt

Match 4 - Copthall Barnet, 6 August 2000 This final and deciding league match was scheduled to be a home fixture at the new Millennium Arena in Battersea Park. But here we were again at Copthall Stadium effectively losing home advantage. The reason for changing venues had been staring us in the face for several weeks. Our new track at Battersea had proved to be deficient in several areas as soon as it opened in late May. Unfortunately the main contractors had been so tardy in rectifying the problem areas that the Temporary Operating Licence had been withdrawn, thereby effectively closing the Stadium to senior competitions. Originally we had been promised that all would be well. Being pessimists at heart, we had also reserved Copthall Stadium - just in case. And as it turned out our contingency plans had to be put into effect with Alan Mead and Don Anderson having to transfer all our electric timing equipment across London to fulfil our home fixture obligations.

As a result of wonderful support for the changed venue from all of the officials, our team managers were able to concentrate on assembling a team capable of repelling what was expected to be a serious and strong challenge from Newham and Essex Beagles. It soon became clear that the search for Olympic qualifying times and other commitments would effectively rule out many of our top men from this meet but positive support was coming from several of our stars.

The first good news came from Dwane Chambers who was looking for as much competition as possible following injury and a late start to the season. He was due to race Maurice Green at Crystal Palace the next day so this was a real bonus. Dwane duly won the 100m easing to a legal 10.31 while Marlon Dickson convincingly won the 'B' 100m in 10.60 and then gained maximum points in the 'B' 200m. The 'A' 200m was entrusted to 17 year old Tim Abeyie who was looking for a World Junior qualifying time and his 21.67 fifth place would have been better but for a poor start and bend. But another fantastic sprint talent for the future. Joining Dwane, Marlon and Tim in the winning sprint relay squad was Chris Lambert fresh back from Harvard University and injury. Chris ran a controlled final straight and looked relieved that his injured leg had survived the test. So practically maximum points in the sprints.

Matt Douglas made a welcome return and looked forward to contesting the opening track event with Duaine Ladejo. Both considered themselves Olympic contenders for the 400mH and were looking for a qualifying time. So it was a surprise when Duaine switched to the 'B' race at the last minute. Both men narrowly missed the target time and were to leave their 'head to head' to the AAA's Championship. Matt gained some consolation from winning the 'B' high hurdles in a time of 14.08 while Neil Owen was back to his classy best winning the 'A' race with a legal sub 14 effort.

Thankfully, Paul Evans also put this match on his priority list and proceeded to run away from a class field in the 5000m for maximum points. Charles Herrington paced himself like a veteran and ran through the field for second spot in the 'B'. Welcome near maximum points in the 5000m was a lift to the team. Soon after Paul added good points in the 1500m while in the 'A' race, Alex Hutchinson ran his last race for the season to place 5th.

But the icing on the cake was the steeplechase, where not only did Kevin Nash return from illness but Justin Chaston, looking splendidly fit, front ran to an easy win. His attendance (by courtesy of Sir Eddie) sent a signal to the opposition that we were in this to win!

Even so the points with Newham were close. Event by event they matched us win for win, second for second and so on. In the hammer Mick Jones' 'A' win and Phil Spivey's 'B' silver were matched by a second and a win. We pulled ahead slightly in the discus with Lee again pipping Kevin Brown. Honours even in the javelin as Mark Roberson secured another Newham win with Stuart Faben third. In the 'B', javelin positions were reversed with Wesley Smith's stunning win with a 60m plus p.b. But it was the shot where Newham finally faltered, as the often erratic Mark Proctor failed to post a distance with three no throws. To rub home the advantage Lee Newman gained silver in the 'A', beating Dave Readle who went on to win the 'B'. And we eased into the lead!

And then our young tigers put the result beyond doubt as first Phillips Idowu doubled the triple and long jumps. A happy Samson Oni finished 4th in the high jump with a World Junior qualifying mark of 2.15m and was only denied victory on countback. Richard Aspden again gained solid points, while in the pole vault Ian Tullett equalled the winning height with a fine 5.20m effort, narrowly beating Ben Flint who was pleased to be competing again after injury.

And then, just as were beginning to celebrate another match and league victory, tragedy struck. In the long relay Geoff Dearman had requested a late leg in the hope he could obtain a sub 46 split. Sure enough he took over in 5th spot and burst into electrifying action. So fast was his start that within 100m he was up to third. A further acceleration was to eat into the lead and then tragedy as a hamstring pulled. Quick first aid minimised the damage but Geoff was out so close to the AAA's and so close to the deadline for gaining the Olympic selection which had seemed a formality.

Victory thereafter was a muted affair. Our first thoughts were of a wonderful competition brightened by so many high class performances. But the sight of Geoff being stretchered off saddened the whole team.

The meeting - our meeting - ran impeccably and we should not conclude this season's league reports without special thanks to Pat Mead for once again getting all the officials out; to Alan Mead and Don Anderson for not only mastering the complicated new electric photo finish equipment but also for setting it up and providing a fast trouble free service and finally John Jeffery, who not only co-managed the team but was also Meeting Director for the day, effortlessly sorting out the numerous problems which arise during every match.

And finally, thanks to all our supporters, both present and future, and above all to our number one fan, Sir Eddie Kulukundis, who attends every meet and who always puts supporting our Teams at the top of his busy and very full schedule.

Report · page 25

Four year grip on Gold Cup slips

Track & Field - British Athletics League Gold Cup

Four year grip on Gold Cup slips

Semi Final - Rugby, 23 July 2000 As Cup holders, we received a particularly kind semi-final draw. There was no other Division 1 Club to face but we knew that second division Blackheath, Cardiff and Swansea could all produce strong teams at one per event. We were not complacent but equally we wished to keep the squad small to save on ever increasing transport costs. As it turned out, Blackheath pipped us by half a point but third place Swansea were well behind. So as usual we eased into the Cup final.

Our six wins came from Geoff Dearman (400m), Jason Lobo (800m), Neil Owen (110mH), Stuart Faben (Javelin) and the sprint relay boys.

Ezra Clark had one of his better triple jump competitions gaining second spot and narrowly fouling a 15m plus effort. Also with two second places was Lee Newman, who had a terrific day throwing a season's best of 17.38m in the shot and having a seemingly good throw of 60m in the discus disallowed by a judge deemed by Lee to be both infirm and myopic. Unfortunately for Lee, he was bested on both occasions by Blackheath's Udechuku. Perhaps unluckiest man was junior Tim Abeyie who clocked 21.3 in the 200m but with a wind just 0.1m/sec over the legal limit.

Matt Douglas did the high jump and both relays. Neil Owen added the hammer to his repertoire and when asked by the judge whether he was a right or left handed thrower, said that he preferred the central delivery. His 7.12m effort was probably pushing the limits of this unpromising technique and anything further would have been decidedly painful! Equally painful was the inability of the team managers to get anybody to run either the steeplechase or 10,000m despite strenuous efforts in the preceding week.

Teams: 1, Blackheath 125; 2, Belgrave 124.5; 3, Swansea 96; 4, Rugby 91.5; 5, Cardiff 83; 6, City of Norwich 76; 7, Telford 62; 8, City of Stoke 50.

Final - Bedford Stadium - 19 August 2000 One week after the AAA's and ten days before the final Olympic qualifying deadline meant that this match would be more important to some than others. Indeed several guest competitors from other Clubs were allowed in the hope they would achieve qualifying performances. In certain events, however, notably the steeplechase, other venues were hosting meetings that removed for example, Justin Chaston from our start list.

So this was always going to be a meeting with surprises - none more so than the strength of the Birchfield squad. Remember Birchfield failed to make the Cup Final at all last year and performed at less than par all season in the League. But here they were, determined to show us that when roused they were practically invincible.

Our start was as usual excellent with a lack lustre win from Mick Jones in the hammer. Mick was still recovering from four week absence due to injury and was spending his time going from meet to meet in search of the big one. He won, but with a distance well below his qualifying target. Charles Herrington opened our track effort with a fine third spot in the 10,000m, winning the South of England title in the process (combined race).

Two guests in the long jump meant that Julian Flynn finished sixth in the competition but fourth in the match. Guest Darren Ritchie headed some impressive performances with a windy 7.90m. Meanwhile, equally impressive performances in the discus where Birchfield's Bob Weir threw a massive 65.08m to clinch his Olympic place. Lee Newman finished third in the match with an impressive 57.44m, ahead of UK record holder Perris Wilkins who seems to under-perform on the big occasions.

Matt Douglas who had qualified for Sydney the week before turned out intending to do enough for max points. Unusually drawn this time in Lane 1, he blasted the first 200m and held on to win comfortably in a huge p.b., and a new Cup Record of 49.38 sec. Nobody was more surprised than Matt and more pleased than his Belgrave team mates and supporters.

Although at this stage we were just about in contention, we knew that lack of competitors in the 1500m and steeplechase would make winning impossible. Additionally, the 400m flat was to provide fewer points than usual although junior Tim Bayley gained useful experience at the highest level.

Marlon Dickson ran exceptionally well in the 100m, his season's best of 10.46 almost good enough for third spot. It was, however, just over the legal limit and seemed to reflect our overall effort which was nearly good enough but not quite.

A final boost came from Ben Challenger who not only won the high jump in his first outing for us this year, but who also ran the glory leg in the sprint relay, claiming a 10.5 sec effort and a 51.0 split in the long relay. Also in the long relay was pole vaulter Ben Flint who clocked 52.0 secs to partly atone for a disappointing 4.50m in the pole vault. He had been held up in traffic, used 4.50m as a warm up but then failed to clear heights which he would normally reach with his eyes shut (well almost!)

And so we finished in fourth spot having surrendered the Trophy in a big way. The only consolation for the management was that in fourth spot we would qualify for seeding in next year's Competition which, it is rumoured, will take place in July 2001, due the early World Championships in Edmonton, Canada which are due to be held at the beginning of August.

Report · page 26

Match 1, Colchester, 29 April 2000.

Catherine Eastham

Track & Field - Women's Southern League Division Three

Reports by Catherine Eastham

Match 1, Colchester, 29 April 2000. Considering the first match of the season was in the midst of the bank holiday season and there were difficulties getting hold of many of our athletes, Belgrave turned out an excellent effort gaining 161.5 points and third place.

Nearly all of the events were covered - the only exceptions being the B strings for the U17 80m hurdles and the hammer. As per usual, the Belles all pulled together and earned points for the team in events they don't usually compete in.

The usual complaint that we didn't have enough juniors couldn't be used this time round. Paul and Kim Collier had done a superb job rounding up the troops and encouraging many new youngsters to join in.

Cherridawn Foster's running is going from strength to strength as shown in her 1st place in the 200m in 28.3. Louise Collier and Georgie Bennett also had good runs in the 800m.

Belgrave's secret weapon could arrive this season in the form of Yinka Odelusi. Yinka launched a shot of 8 metres 15 in her first competition for the Belles, finishing 3rd.

A few 'new' faces appeared for the seniors. Although only new in the sense that we haven't seen them competing in the Ladies Southern League in recent years! Ann Walters clocked 13.20 in the 3000m to finish second, and then competed in the shot, discus and javelin! Jane Searle, now first claim for Belgrave came third in the 800m and followed with the first leg in the 400m relay.

Jennifer Culley stormed home to win the 400m in 57.1 seconds, then joined Julie Bernard, Syreeta Stracey and Bianca Casey to finish second in the 4 x 100m.

All in all, whether down to the good weather, a great team spirit, or the excellent performances on the day, it was a good start to the season. Keep up the good work Belles.

Report · page 26

Match 2, Tooting, 20 May 2000

Catherine Eastham

The second match saw the Belles competing on home turf. Sadly though this meant Tooting as the new Battersea stadium wasn't quite ready. The Belles finished a place higher in this match and second place moved the team up to 9th place in the league. Promotion was still a possibility!

A strong team turned out and all the events were covered except for both of the U17 hurdle events. Paul and Kim Collier's ability to turn out an ever increasing and eager team of girls seems to be improving match upon match. The performances of the girls are improving too, and the Belles triumphed with a number of first places - Susanna Murray-Burton's B string 100m, Cherridawn Foster's and Louise Collier's A and B string 200m, the 4 x 100m relay team: Cherridawn, Susan, Catherine Olawore and Louise Collier, and Belinda Cobden-Ramsey and Louise Collier's 800ms.

Louise earned herself a pet hamster "PB", having broken the U13 girls 800m record with the time of 2.35.4.

The seniors did rather well again, with the usual suspects multi-tasking yet again!

Top performances went to Nicki Haines and Rachel Weston placing 1st and 2nd in the 3000m. Rachel then teamed up with Danielle Kandaros, Tanya Sturton and Syreeta Stracey to deliver a 2nd place in the 4 x 400m relay.

Other 1st and 2nd placings went to Bianca Casey and Jacqui Smiter in the high jump, Harriet Payne and Danielle Kandaros in the long jump, and Jacqui and Danielle in the 400m hurdles.

We were still missing Vicki and Kelly in the throws but nonetheless covered all the events with a combination of Ann Walters tripling up on the shot, discus and javelin, Angela Weah's return to the field since becoming a mum, Tania having a go at the javelin, and Cynthia in the hammer. Gail Porter also popped by for a quick hammer throw on her way to work!

Report · page 27

Match 6, Sutcliffe Park, 2 September 2000

What a multi-eventer Maureen Knight could be. This time she went on a points scoring spree and ended up with four first places and a second. In the triple jump Michelle Nestor pushed her all the way though with both of them setting personal best distances. There was a terrific tussle in the 3000 metres where Tania Sturton and Syreeta Stracey battled to be the first Belgrave girl home and as a result overtook most of the rest of the field. Syreeta eventually had the best finishing burst but their efforts had taken them both into 2nd place.

As far as the team competition was concerned, British League club Aldershot were too strong for the rest and in a tough match the Belles were 4th. So, the season was over and we were finally placed 11th in the league. Our only failing was an inability to get all the best competitors out together. The team spirit is good and if we missed out on promotion in 2000, surely it will come soon.

Report · page 27

Match 4, Salisbury, 15 July 2000

Maureen Knight made her first appearance in the team for the Salisbury match and immediately picked up a win in the high jump and gained further good points in the long jump and javelin. Star of the day was Jennifer Culley, however, winner of 100, 200 and 400 metre A races. The throws were strong, thanks to Kelly Mellis, Gail Porter and Vicki Hassell and in the younger age group Natasha Richardson and Cherridawn Foster kept the pressure up with winning performances. The match was closely fought and the Belles really needed a victory to have any hope of promotion in 2000 but in the end, second place was an excellent effort.

Report · page 27

Match 3, Hayes, 24 June 2000

With the absence of Cynthia's team management skills, through watching her daughter wed, and with a number of illnesses and injuries spread amongst the team, this always looked like it would be a tricky match.

With a number of key team members being uncontactable in the weeks beforehand, the already stretched team was stretched beyond belief when there were several 11th hour pull-outs.

Up against clubs in 2nd and 7th places it meant that our best was going to have to be better than usual. However, with several athletes covering the maximum number of events, many of which they didn't specialise in, it didn't look likely that we would succeed.

Finishing in fifth place is perhaps the worst result the Belles have encountered for some time, and disappointing though this was, some relief came from the fact that many other clubs across the league appeared to have equally bad days and we ended up dropping just one place overall in the league.

Despite the doom and gloom there were some star performances of the day: Julie Bernard improved upon both her 100 metres and 400 metres relay times to set two PB's for the day, and Louise Collier clocked 2.33.6 in the 800 metres which brought her under the England Schools qualifying time. Performances from a number of our girls were improving, with Susanna Murray-Burton landing 4.20 in the long jump and Belinda Cobden-Ramsey throwing 17.39 in the javelin. Susanna's 100 metres time is so consistent that for the 4th match in the season she clocked 14.0!

Best performances so far in the league, after three matches, went to Kelly Mellis in the discus with 38.90 metres, Jennifer Culley 12.5 in the 100 metres, Helen Maskrey 5.06.5 in the 1500 metres, Jacqui Smiter 1.90 in the pole vault and the 4 x 400 metres team clocking 4.25.0. We just need to be more consistent and field a full turnout and we could be up there with the best.

Report · page 27

Match 5, Norman Park, 12 August 2000

Another second team place came at Bromley but it could so easily have been a win as we ended up just 5.5 points behind Chelmsford. Louise Collier was back to winning ways in the under 15 800 metres and was backed up well by Belinda Cobden-Ramsey who was first home in the 'B' event. Perhaps the best performance came from 15 year-old Michelle Nestor who went over 10 metres for the first time in the triple jump to place 2nd in the senior competition. Three first places went to Maureen Knight and the points were piling up but, missing a few specialist middle-distance runners, in the end it was the Essex club that just prevailed while the Belles kept just 4 points clear of the home club Blackheath.

Report · page 30

Belles return to Cup competition

Track & Field - Women's Southern League Division Three & Jubilee Cup

Belles return to Cup competition

Jubilee Cup First Round, Basingstoke, 11 June 2000. Having not competed in the Jubilee Cup for a while, the Belles entered Round 1 eager and willing to make their mark. However, despite being in second place after nine events, winning the match somehow seemed out of reach.

The random match draw didn't go our way. Whilst Match 1 often only had 3 out of 6 teams competing, Match 2 consistently pulled out a full quota of athletes. As quoted on the Round 1 programme though, the random match draw did create an aura of mystery and the unknown and was appreciated by most clubs!

Still, although we came home without the cup, we made some positive progress and boosted the confidence of our team in its ability to compete with higher division clubs.

Before praising all the athletes, a big thanks has to go to Bob Bridges. Not content with running after the athletes he trains and managing the blokes' team, Bob became joint team manager and minibus driver supremo for the day. Thanks Bob, we'd never even have made it to round one without your help. Thanks also to Eric and Mavis Hall for their support and Richard Hearn for timekeeping and hurdle carrying.

Faced with only one athlete per event we had assumed it would be quite easy to pull together a full team, but injuries, illnesses and other commitments put paid to that dream! Nevertheless, all events were covered, even though a little bit of persuasion was needed by the time we reached the 4 x 400m and we knew we weren't going to win.

For the first time this season both Vicki Hassell and Kelly Mellis were available to throw. Vicki covered the Hammer and Javelin and Kelly the Shot and Discus. Both made their mark on the competition and Kelly achieved our only first place of the day with 38.48 in the discus.

Discovery of the day was Michelle Nestor, who we're pleased to find is a secret triple jumper. Michelle's triple jumping was a regular fixture for the rest of the Southern League fixtures. Another great find was Julie Bernard who, at the last minute, was persuaded to run the final leg of the 4 x 400m relay. Julie is quite new to Belgrave and had so far only competed up to 200 metres. Her relay performance guaranteed her a place in the relay if not the flat 400m for the rest of the season.

Notice · page 32

SOUTHERN COUNTIES VETERANS' AC TRACK & FIELD LEAGUE

Maureen Jones

Maureen Jones writes: Due to extremely short notice of the fixture dates they could not be included in the 2000 fixture card so both women's and men's teams got off to a poor start - and things didn't improve all season. Brian Holmes posted some extremely good results but became a one man team. He was selected for "The Best of the Rest" team for the final at Hendon, which he enjoyed, but he would have been happier to have been part of a Belgrave team. So, next summer I will try to persuade more of the oldies to compete and I will endeavour to obtain the finalised dates for the fixture card. Any men over 40 or women over 35 can compete in middle-distance, sprints, jumps or throws so please contact me if you would like to increase our numbers. Maureen's number is [phone number withheld].

Maureen JonesBrian Holmes

Report · page 32

A trip to Paavo Nurmi Land

Arthur Bruce

Arthur Bruce reports on the European Veterans' Championship 6-16 July, Jyvaskyla, Finland

A small but select group departed Stansted Airport at the unearthly time of 7.30am for Helsinki enroute to the university town of Jyvaskyla, about 200 miles north of the Finnish capital, set among vast coniferous forests and endless lakes. The excellent "Buzz" flight set us down at the capital on schedule and was uneventful save for Fred Paget picking up a poor 60 years old waif and stray also on his way to the Championships. Overflowing with the milk of human kindness Fred invited him to join us at the summer accommodation about 2 miles from the main stadium. He proved to be a member of Chelmsford AC and a veritable Walter Mitty capable of almost anything!

The Championships, spread over ten days and set in two fine modern stadiums, provided non-stop competition in age groups 40 to 80 for men and 35 to 80 for women. The racing proved highly competitive with an increasing high standard, especially for the UK athletes.

Noteworthy among the younger winners was Stephen Peters aged 47 with a treble in 100, 200 and 400 in 10.9, 22.3 and 50 seconds. Probably one of the best double came from the Irish Champion with a win in the 800 metres in 1:52 and the 1500 metres with a last lap of 59 seconds; he bettered his 800 metres on returning to the UK with a performance of 1:50 in a BMC meeting. We must of course enthuse over our own Charlie Dickinson who, after gaining bronze in the 10,000 metres giving a gutty performance behind a really good Russian, came back four days later to win the 5,000 metres in 16:11 against a strong field. His burst two laps from home turned the tables on his conquerer from the longer race and his last mile took only 5:08! Not bad for a venerable 52.

Some of the older competitors gave heartwarming performances and were often involved in four or five events, setting fine examples for younger athletes. We returned thrilled and refreshed after a feast of excellent athletics.

M50 10000m (7 July): 3, C.Dickinson 34:24.81. M50 5000m (12 July): 1, C.Dickinson 16:11.39.

Arthur BruceFred PagetCharlie DickinsonC. DickinsonChas.Stephen PetersVan NotenKotnikFred

Report · page 33

We are the Champions!

John Powell retires from Belgrave Junior Team Management at the top

Birmingham, 10 September 2000. Belgrave Harriers lifted the National Junior League Championship on Sunday for the first time in the club's history after a humdinger of a national final, at the Alexander Stadium, Birmingham.

Team Captain James Tuson, and team manager John Powell, who now retires after 25 years of youth team management, were presented with the Champions Trophy by British Athletics Under-20's Performance Director, Brian Hall, after over seven hours of thrilling athletics.

Early disappointments had suggested an uphill struggle for Belgrave in the league's showcase event of the year, but the team rallied together to muster a furious finish.

NATIONAL FINAL

"I really thought we'd blown it early on," said John, "but there was the kind of determination out there today that was the mark of champions. It has been a season of very dramatic ups and downs, but this result left me so proud of the team - they earned every point by sheer graft and determination."

The team award was not Belgrave's only triumph either, with Tim Abeyie winning the individual 'Grand Prix' final after outstanding performances in the 100m, 200m and long jump. He won the 100m in 10.7s, won the A-string 200m in 21.68 - beating the AAA silver medallist Ben Lewis into the bargain - and managed a 5.48m long jump. He totalled 2572 points.

As if that were not enough, Belgrave's Scot Thompson was second with 2449, and John Osazuwa was voted Athlete of the Match after his outstanding hammer throw of 64.56m which fell less than 20cm short of the national league record.

The real trump card in Belgrave's pack was saved right to the last. Tim Abeyie - exhausted after three individual events and the sprint relay, which Belgrave also won - lined up on anchor leg of the 4x400m relay.

Belgrave battled round to hand over to him in fourth spot after Herculean efforts by Jonathan Simpson, Denny de Emmony and Timothy Bayley. Abeyie blasted off and almost immediately caught the leading group. In a thrilling finish he elevated the team to second place with a stunning split time of 47.8 seconds - a time which began to bring him into the reckoning for a place on the World Junior Championships team.

Another athlete desperately trying to win over the selectors was British Junior Captain, Chris' Lambert. His second-placed 10.68 ended up not being enough, but he travelled back across the Atlantic to Harvard University content in the knowledge that his six-month hamstring injury problems do now seem to be well and truly behind him.

Supporting Lambert and Abeyie was Darren Chin, who scored more than handy points in both B-string sprints. He was second in the 100m in 10.78, and won the 200m in 23.0.

In the 400m Tim Bayley narrowly missed out on a medal in taking fourth in the B-race in 49.27, but made amends in the 800m with a fine win in 1:56.03.

In the 110m hurdles Samson Oni added gold in the B-string, clocking 16.00, to his A-string gold in the high jump after clearing 2.05.

Scot Thompson enjoyed another outstanding afternoon in all four throws. He was second in the A-string discus with a throw of 50.04, set a new personal best in winning the shot with 16.28, won the B-string hammer with 48.88, and added fourth in the B javelin with 45.12.

Team captain James Tuson played his part in winning the B-string high jump clearing 1.90, and

continued at foot of next page >>>

NATIONAL JUNIOR LEAGUE

was fourth in the B-string triple jump with 12.96.

The final relay events were held amid an electric atmosphere with Belgrave's quartet of Darren Chin, Tim Abeyie, Daniel Buss and Chris' Lambert wining the 4x100m in 41.91. Belgrave's victory was then sealed by the 4x400m quartet anchored by Tim Abeyie.

Belgrave's juniors will now go on to represent Great Britain in the European Nations Junior Cup Final in 2001.

Report · page 34

... but Young Athletes face the drop

Track & Field - National Young Athletes' League Southern Premier Division

... but Young Athletes face the drop

One of the great difficulties in age group athletics is keeping the momentum going. Every year one has to address the problem of athletes going up out of the top of the age bands to become seniors. Although we often welcome new competitors who are 14, 15, 16 years of age, the only way to make sure that we have a full squad at all times is to go out every single year and recruit a completely new batch of under 13s. We do this mostly by organising a schools meeting very early in the season but it does take a tremendous amount of effort by an extremely small group of organisers. We owe a tremendous debt to Jon Lambert and his family and Kim and Paul Collier who are currently bearing the brunt of this work.

Last season was particularly disappointing for them as the team filled 3rd or 4th spot in each match and as a result, by the end of the summer, just fell into the relegation zone of the Southern Premier Division. We will now take up a place in Division One South in 2001.

It's not going to be easy bounce back. The standard is tremendously high at this level as well. How Enfield & Haringey manage it goodness only knows. Not only did they win the Southern Premier Division but their 'B' team topped the next division down as well. Not surprising then that they went on to victory in the National Final.

But come on you youngsters. Our team managers and coaches are right behind you and we have some great young captains. We can get up there again.

Jon LambertKimPaul Collier

Feature · page 35

A SICK MANN

Pete Mulholland

On a day the British press were carrying the narrative of IAAF suspensions, the Sun newspaper was banner headlining an exclusive story on the fate of Belgrave Harrier Lionel Mann, writes Pete Mulholland. Sharing the front page with Big Brother's 'Nasty Nick' and Posh Spice, it was disclosed that Mann, Chief Chef to Her Majesty the Queen, had been struck down by salmonella with the paper disclosing, "The world-famous chef has been banned from the royal kitchens until he's better." According to a courtier, "The Queen has asked to be kept closely informed of his condition." Said to have contracted the bug during a trip to Mexico prior to his current tenure at Balmoral, the 59-year-old Mann, a former Belgrave President, will probably be more concerned that his training and racing activities will have to come to a halt. According to a colleague, "He's a serial racer." We are happy to report, of course that Lionel is now completely recovered and his name has now been relegated to the latter pages of the Belgravian in the results roundup.

Lionel MannPete MulhollandMannLionel

Report · page 36

Solid Surrey Silver

Road & Country

SURREY ROAD RELAY CHAMPIONSHIPS WIMBLEDON PARK, 9 SEP 2000

Solid Surrey Silver

You couldn't ask much more for the start of the "winter" season - twenty senior runners, a veterans team, a nice crowd of spectators and a general feeling that good times lie ahead. Well, alright, a win would have been nice, but all credit to a South London Harriers outfit that turned out their strongest team to carry the day. The Bels. broke the old course record - but SLH had already scorched home over 2 minutes ahead.

Several of Belgrave's Surrey team were in far from the best of shape, Roger Alsop and Alaster Stewart just about overcoming injury, Yacin Yusuf only recently returning to running after sickness and Graham Adams and Jim Estall both having had low key summers. It was a similar story among the non-Surrey men where even Paul Freary had a fair amount of fitness to reclaim - even though he only just missed Alsop's Belgrave best of 14:23 from 1999.

Cap'n Rog. enjoyed the first event of his stag weekend, going some 15 seconds faster than anticipated to bring the Bels home to 3rd on stage 1, while Rick Hayman was only 10 seconds behind for the 'B's. Meanwhile South London had grabbed a lead they were never to relinquish and Reigate Priory were in second.

Twenty-one year-old Kevin Quinn was in sparkling form on stage 2 to chop a minute off his best time round the course (he had recently recorded personal records of 1:56 and 3:59 on the track). Steve Clarke for the 'B's went past him up the hill but Kev was in no mood to give way and came back at his club mate once at the top. Steve's time was Belgrave's second fastest of the day but both runners had to give way to Herne Hill's Boyle.

Stage 3 and an out of condition Yacin Yusuf pulled the Bels back to 2nd while Rob McHarg advanced the 'B' team's cause by going into 3rd. Up front Gary Staines set the day's fastest. It was good to see "Gerry" Adams back in the team with a solid run but Joachim Wolf took 2 seconds out of him to put the non-Surrey men just 7 seconds down.

Veteran Jim Estall gained a little ground on the leaders on the penultimate stage and handed over to Alaster Stewart whose run was another good effort after an injury-plagued summer. An unfit Simon Elliott struggled for the 'B's on stage 5 and Herne Hill moved into third. Paul Freary set the fastest Belgrave time of the day to try to get the 'B' squad back into the frame and although he caught up two whole minutes, he ran out of road with a seven second gap remaining.

20 teams started, 16 teams finished.

Report · page 37

Upward trend continues

Road & Country

SEAA 6 x 6 kms ROAD RELAY RUSHMOOR ARENA, ALDERSHOT, 1 OCT 2000

Upward trend continues

The upward trend in our running fortunes continued at Rushmoor Arena, near Aldershot, when the club managed to finish three teams in the South of England AA 6-Stage Road Relay Championship and claim a best ever team performance in the event. And while the 'A' squad were just out of the medals, the Bels. second team proved to be the first 'B' team home in 14th - a position that our first team have only ever beaten once before. With a third team mostly made up of new members and two-thirds of a 'D' team also in action, the future is beginning to look distinctly exciting.

Borough of Hounslow AC were back in force for the event, going to the lengths of wearing t-shirts bearing the legend "The Boys Are Back In Town", dying their hair in club colours of blue and white and wearing face paints of the same hue. They just had to win dressed like that and from Sam Haughian's initial leg until the end they just went further and further away. Behind them the order chopped and changed until the Thames boys surprised by forcefully claiming 2nd at the halfway stage with the always-strong Aldershot team moving into 3rd at the same point. The Bels. gradually saw off the challenges of London Irish, Blackheath, Southampton Runners and South London and 4th place at the end was about what we expected.

Charles Herrington set the race up nicely by placing 7th on the opening stage among teams that were mostly never going to go the whole distance at that level but the rest of the team, although occupying the race positions we were expecting, were all about 20 seconds down on predicted times. All had a lot more fitness to find - which was no bad thing at that early stage of the season.

Rob McHarg ran himself into the 'six' to compete for Belgrave in the Irish Road Relay Championships three weeks later and two who impressed greatly in the 'C' team were the French U20 steeplechaser Fabien Bernard-Gaudin and Norwegian Knut Hegvold.

88 teams started; 66 teams finished.
